Founders' provisions neither count nor cover what is kept: they carry a band to dependable harvests (05-06 §5.4). A hut takes no more than half the building time its household can give before its roof deadline (a tuning value), and no more than the program's largest, 4 m of radius and about 50 m², the top of Glastonbury's roundhouses (10-06 §2.1). A household that has a home builds a new one only when its means pay for all of one (all of it is beyond its needs) at least a quarter larger in floor area (a tuning value; 10-06 §5.4 has households extend and rebuild, and gives no threshold), as large as its members need: it builds beside the old, lives there until the new roof is on, then moves in and the old hut comes down. Wealth buys floor area alone, since the hut is one round room; 10-06 §1.2 finds wealth buying common and reception space more than sleeping room, which needs grammar v2 (M3b). Rafters are priced at one length, so the largest huts are a little cheap to build. A household checks its means once a day; at 238 ms a day against 235 for the village of 302 above, the cost does not show.
- **NUDGE: house size by wealth is silent so far (2026-10-04, M3a slice L):** in six-year runs of four seeds under both regimes (40-person bands), the floor-area Gini and the floor per house stayed within the spread of runs without the rule: 0.12–0.22 and 26–31 m² at the sixth year's end, against 0.15–0.22 and 25–29 m². Seed 2's households held goods worth 700–2,900 hours of their work in its fifth and sixth years, 70–1,740 of them to spare. A hut costs 450 hours for two to 1,050 for the largest (703 for five), cutting and carrying included, and the least a household in a hut of 26 m² would build again costs 780; so 0.3 of the most anyone could spare, about 520 hours, paid for no new home, and the few new couples brought little to spare to their first. House sizes follow household size at building. More surplus (better yields, livestock), huts that age and must be rebuilt (M3b's age and upkeep), or second buildings would give wealth occasions to show; none is forced here.
- **The morning's water (2026-10-04, found by the smoke):** with slice L's changes the 30-day smoke failed one river-valley world in about 35 runs: a household had no water at 06:00. Traced in a world that repeated it (1 in 17 tries): a household of four fetched 60 litres at noon, about 18 hours' worth, which ran out at 05:58; a member went for water after the morning meal, at 05:55, and was back at 06:01. The dusk rule above held that water lasting until sunrise lasted the night. Water at home must now last until sunrise and then through the first meal and the trip to the water; a household short of that fetches before dark. After the change no household went dry in 40 tries of that world, and 50 smoke worlds passed in a row.
- **Grain carried in store (2026-10-04, M3a slice L, research 08-05 §1.6; ADR-0006 §4):** the smoke's new price check found grain asked the same before the harvest as after: an ask moves from a cost anchor, and grain's own cost was the work of growing it whatever the season. A kilogram held in spring had to be grown as more than a kilogram the autumn before, since the store takes its share (08-05 §1.6: storage links harvest prices to later ones). A grown good's own cost is now its growing cost carried from the day its crop starts to ripen to the day it is reckoned, at its half-life under the household's roof or in the open: grain is cheapest as it ripens and dearest just before. Offers, purchases and the wealth measures' prices all use it. Under a roof (5 % a year) asks came out about 4 log points higher before the harvest than after, in every world of the ten-year smoke; the research's markets show 17–61 (16-01 §2.2), with the credit costs, risk and scarcity premiums the model does not have. ADR-0006 §4's other term, a review that also moves from the seller's stock against expected sales, is still not implemented. Two firm tests assumed the first workshop opened was the one they set up, and that a workshop faked idle could not sell again before its review; with other households now opening workshops too they failed now and then, and they now follow their own workshop and fake its idleness just before each night's reviews.
- **Smoke checks of the village economy (2026-10-04, M3a slice L, research 16-01, 08-14 §3.1, §3.2):** the smoke worlds take the content's property regimes in turn by seed. The ten-year run samples each settlement at the start of each month (the food its households hold, and what each seller asks for the crop's grain), and checks at each year's end that land is held and worked as the regime says (`claims_problems`: holders that exist and are of the regime's kind, leases only where it lets land and from a household to another, and under holders no field worked by another household without a lease), that no field was lost or moved, and that the latest wealth measures are numbers in their ranges that fit the regime; each of these fails a world. At the end it grades: **stocks**, whether food held rose from just before the crop ripens to just after the settlement's harvest is in, in each year after its first (red only if never); **asks**, the mean gap between the grain asked before and after (green above 2 log points; gray with fewer than two harvests to compare); **inequality**, the Gini of goods from the fifth year (amber below 0.3); and **workshops**, whether the hours worked for each over its life are right-skewed, the mean above the median (gray with fewer than ten). §4.7's band of 0.3–0.75 for the wealth Gini, a failure there, belongs to M3c's dashboard of 50-year runs: early farming need not show its inequality within ten years (08-14 §3.2: substantial variation, no immediate jump), so here a lower Gini is amber, reported and not hidden. The first ten-year run (all ten worlds passed, 10 min 41 s): stocks and asks green in all ten; inequality amber in all ten, Gini of goods 0.04–0.26 by the end; workshops green in four, amber in two (mean equal to the median), gray in four. Seven of the ten bands lived on; the bands of seed 1 failed in both landscapes, as before, and that of the coast's seed 4 died out in its tenth year.
- **The M3a demo (2026-10-04, M3a slice L, plan §7):** seed 2 (river valley, 6 km map), lived from the command line (`civ-host run`, which now reports each year's prices too, and with `--band` and `--families` makes a village of hundreds) under household fields and under village fields, three runs of each since runs differ; no seed was picked. **A band of 40, ten years:** at the tenth year's end, under household fields and village fields: 48–51 and 47–49 people, 12 households each; Gini of goods 0.15–0.19 and 0.16–0.18, of land worked 0.09–0.12 and 0.10, of land held 0.09–0.12 and 0 (the village held all 18–18.5 ha); floor area 0.21–0.22 in both and 27–28 m² a house; the top tenth 15–18% and 15–17% of the goods; 540–560 and 552–574 hours of goods a head; 0–1 field let and none (the regime lets none); a sickle sold for 3.6–3.9 and 3.6–4.4 hours of work, a hoe 2.3–2.5 and 2.5; grain asked at 1.87 in all as the year turned; no money anywhere; 8–24 and 15–28 workshops given up over the years. **A village of hundreds** (a band of 125 joined by 20 families, 214 people at the founding): at its fifth year's end 245–250 and 246–254 people in 59 households, with the same Ginis of goods (0.14–0.15 and 0.15), floor area (0.18) and floor per house (26 m²) under both; under household fields three fields were let for 99–155 kg of grain in rent. In its sixth year the harvest came in 37–41% short of the year before (35–38 t against about 60) and hunger followed under both. Under village fields 82–105 people died over the year and 5–7 left, leaving 154–175 at its end, with a Gini of goods of 0.27–0.32. Under household fields 25–73 died, but households left sooner (0, 129 and 229 people), leaving 189, 90 and 12, with Ginis of 0.11–0.24, and up to 38 fields let. Households left sooner under household fields and died more under village fields; why has not been traced. In the seventh year every household of every run left the valley.
- **NUDGE: the regimes part only under scarcity (2026-10-04, M3a slice L):** with land plentiful, households under either regime work what they need, and nothing else in the economy depends on who holds the ground; the regimes came out the same but for who holds land and the few fields let (research 08-14 §2.2: where households can get an independent livelihood, holders have little hold over them). Only a famine parted them. A village crowded onto too little land for long enough, with people who stay, would test whether tenure shapes wealth; that needs villages that survive scarcity (next NUDGE). Nothing was tuned to make them part.
- **NUDGE: a village of hundreds made at once does not last (2026-10-04, M3a slice L):** a band of 125 joined by 20 families never fed itself. Ground is broken only in the spring window, at 1,000 hours a hectare for new ground (plus 1,000 for woodland), so its fields grew from 35 to 81 ha in five years while its households needed about 100 (0.40 ha a head for a year's food at a cautious yield, 10-01 §2.3); it worked 0.31 ha a head and lived on what it carried and one good year, holding about four months' grain going into its sixth. Adults spent some ten hours a day resting or at the hearth, so labour was not short. A band of 40 has the same shortfall, but the valley's wild food covers far more of it. Likely remedies, for M3c's farming: breaking ground outside the sowing season, as farmers clear in autumn and winter, and reserves for more than the coming year (08-14 §3.7 proposes 30–180 days of reserve to test). Villages of 200–500 run at full detail (slice L's speed), but not for long.
- **M3b design (2026-10-04, plan §7):** four research briefs (knowledge and technique; buildings and grammar v2; structural rules, failure and upkeep; style, earthworks and deposits) from about 40 reports, their key citations checked against the reports. Six slices, M to R (§7). Three ADRs, the share §9's *M3 in three parts* set aside for M3b: [ADR-0008](decisions/0008-knowledge-carried-by-people.md) (knowledge carried by people), [ADR-0009](decisions/0009-building-components.md) (grammar v2, condition and trust) and [ADR-0010](decisions/0010-ground-people-change.md) (deposits and earthworks). Choices that depart from the plan's first sketch: discovery is a hazard on a session's qualified hours, drawn at its end and tied to the person working (07-01 §5.3, §5.5), not the yearly roll from population and specialists of §4.4 and §5.6 (07-01 §1.4 warns against population multipliers); a building's margin is kept per component group and decays with exposure, not one margin per building decaying with age (§5.1; 11-06 §5.1, §5.7); and technique trust is kept per settlement, which stands in for its culture until cultures exist (M5). Style needs no ADR: its traits are realised decisions in the spec's parameters, and taste is behaviour state.
- **Knowledge carried by people, as built (2026-10-04, M3b slice M, ADR-0008):** content API 9 adds the `technique` kind (a name, what a competent person can do, its skill, prerequisites as alternative routes, what discovery will need, `learn_h` and `upbringing`). Activities, recipes and building programs name the technique they need, and an activity that works a recipe needs the recipe's. The people profile's `[knowledge]` table gives the founders' shares, learners per teacher and the learning term. The validator adds E3005 (a technique gates nothing), E3006 (prerequisites in a cycle) and E3007 (a recipe that can never be worked because an input or tool comes only from recipes that need it: 07-03 §6's bootstrap test, as a fixpoint over the goods the world renews). Saves schema 13 keeps each person's entries in `People` (by technique id) and each settlement's record in a new `know` section; saves before it load with everyone given what a founder of their age would bring, and the records begin at the load. Wire 1.13 adds the techniques to `Welcome`, a person's entries to `PersonInfo`, `knowledge_rev` to the snapshot, the `GetKnowledge` query and the `IntroduceTechnique` command. Appended, never renumbered: four chronicle kinds (found, learnt, lost, introduced) and two decision reasons (*learning it from someone who knows*, and *does not know how, and nobody to learn from is at it*).
- **The founders' repertoire (2026-10-04, slice M):** eight techniques gate today's work: growing emmer, grinding at a quern, pounding grain, baking flatbread, knapping sickle blades, shaping stone, shaping wood and building roundhouses (research 07-04, 07-05, 07-07). Every founder old enough for each one's work brings it (share 1.0; 06-08 §3: founders arrive with substantial practical knowledge), and all are learnt at home (06-08 §1.1). Hours that teach them, all tuning values within 07-02 §2.3's 20–200 for a narrow procedure: 20 for baking, pounding and grinding, 100 for growing emmer and shaping wood, 200 for knapping, shaping stone and building roundhouses. A teacher takes two learners at once (07-02 §2.3: one to three), and learning beside someone adds 2 utility points to the work (about what useful work is worth; a tuning value).
- **Upbringing at the work's age (2026-10-04, slice M, ADR-0008 §4):** a child in a household where someone knows a technique learns it on reaching the youngest age of the work it gates: at their first such work or at the next day's start, so the gate never holds a child back from work their household would have them do. The window closes when they could keep a household (15), and work first done later (making an axe or a quern, from 16) is learnt in the year before: nobody leaves home without their household's work. The year is a tuning value. The first version closed the window at 15 for every technique, so nobody born in a world came to know shaping stone. The ten-year smoke's new knowledge check caught it: in each of the first four worlds to finish, some of those old enough for it did not know it (22 of 31 in one village's tenth year). A second version opened a year's window at the work's age (16 to 17). With it, all ten worlds passed the knowledge check, but one coastal world failed the roof check, 7 of 9 households roofed against 80%. One run cannot say whether gating played a part, since runs differ. But it left a known gap: someone keeping a household from 15, an orphan heading it say, would never learn shaping stone. The third version closes the gap.
- **What a settlement knows is derived when asked (2026-10-04, slice M, ADR-0008 §2, §5):** nothing is stored per settlement but its record of when each technique came and went. A death or departure looks over the settlement's residents for each technique the person knew, and the knowledge query derives each settlement's knowers, learners and those who have heard of a technique the same way. In villages of hundreds this costs nothing that shows; an index from technique to knowers waits until it does. ADR-0008 is amended to say so.
- **Slice M changes no behaviour, exactly (2026-10-04):** with the core content every founder knows everything and children learn it all at home, so gating work by knowledge must leave every world's life as it was. A test saves a band, loads the save with the core content and with a copy in which no work names a technique, and lives 120 days in both: everyone does the same things in the same places, the chronicle tells the same story but for knowledge, and every saved section but people's knowledge and the content is byte for byte the same. A child reaches the age of crop work half a day after the save. Removing the upbringing at the first work and the daily upbringing makes it fail. An ignored variant lives ten years (`cargo test -p civ-sim --test knowledge -- --ignored`, about seven minutes): it passed, and at its end everyone old enough for each technique's work knew it.
- **Slice M's review (2026-10-04):** a review of the slice found no critical defect and five smaller ones, all silent with the core content, where everyone knows everything.
  - **Fixed:**
    - A household's own cost of a good now takes each recipe at the skill of the best member who knows it, not of anyone (ADR-0008's consequence).
    - A teacher's two learners now count those on their way to the work.
    - A technique lost when a household leaves now counts the buildings it leaves standing as made with it.
    - A faster way of a farm task that the person does not know no longer rules out a slower one they do.
    - Content may hold at most 65,535 techniques, the 16-bit index people and saves use.
  - **Kept as a simplification:** a session of learning begun beside a teacher counts whole, though the teacher may finish first. Presence is checked when the learner chooses the work. With half of founders knowing each craft, 76 % of learning sessions ended after the teacher's.
- **Slice M in the ten-year smoke (2026-10-04):** with the third upbringing rule, before the review's fixes, all ten worlds passed, the new knowledge check included. 8 of the 10 villages kept at least 10 people: on the coast 18, 45, 28 and 57, and in the river valley 57, 20, 45 and 55. The bands of seed 1 fell to 3 and 0, as in every run since M1. A run of the build before slice M, made alongside, also passed all ten and kept 7: on the coast 15, 44 and 60, and in the river valley 52, 36, 46 and 57. One run each, and runs differ.
- **Discovery, as built (2026-10-04, M3b slice N, ADR-0008 §3):** at the end of each work session, each technique the session can find is a draw. A session finds a technique its activity counts toward (`tried_in`), or the one a session of trying was aimed at. The person must not know it, must know one of its routes of prerequisites wholly, and must have the goods it needs at home (the gate G). The draw is keyed by world seed, person, technique and the session's start, against 1 − exp(−ln 2 · qualified hours / E50) (07-01 §5.3). Qualified hours are 1 % of a routine session's hours (`experiment_share`, 07-01 §2.3) and all of a session of trying, three times for someone already aware of the technique (`aware_try_factor`, 07-02 §1.7's way back; a tuning value). The finder knows it at once. The chronicle names the kind of find: the first anyone made, the first here of what is known elsewhere, of what others here already knew (an independent find), or of what was lost here before. The settlement's record notes it if nobody there knew it. Content API 10, saves schema 14 (when each person last tried, and a session's technique), no wire change: the new decision reasons and the `try` behaviour reach the observer through the welcome's tables.
- **Trying, as built (2026-10-04, slice N):** a new behaviour, `try`, spends an hour at home toward the technique whose problem would cost the household the most food. The candidates are the techniques the person does not know and could find. Its worth is `w_try` (2.0, a tuning value) times the problem's share: food at risk over food at risk plus the trip half-worth of a household's days (the measure gathering trips use). Problems under 5 % of that draw no attention (a tuning value: a problem nobody has gets none, ADR-0008 §3). A person tries at most once a week (`try_gap_days` 7). That is about 2 % of working time, inside 07-01 §2.3's tested 0–5 % and 07-11 §2.2's 0–10 % protected time.
  - **The one problem built: food that will spoil before it is eaten.** It is computed forward from what the household holds rather than looked back on, so it needs no new state. A stock S of a good with decay rate λ, eaten at the household's daily need c after the foods that keep less long, loses S − (c/λ)·ln(1 + λS/c).
  - **Waiting for a technique that needs them:** ADR-0008 §3's other measures, hours spent on a task and work waiting on a good.
- **Preserving (2026-10-04, slice N):** a recipe preserves when its single food input keeps at least four times less long than what it makes (a tuning value). Drying meat (3 days) into dried meat (180) qualifies, while grinding grain into flour does not. A household makes it as much as would otherwise spoil, by the same closed form, when that is at least half a day of one person's food (a tuning value). Its worth is the food kept, weighed as food made ready is (`w_food`, with the trip half-worth).
- **Drying and smoking (2026-10-04, slice N, 07-04 P03; 07-03 §3.5):** not in the founders' repertoire, so a world finds it or not. It is found from hunting and fishing (1 % of their hours) and from trying at spoiling meat and fish, with firewood at home. E50 is 2,000 qualified hours, a near technique within 07-01 §2.3's tested 10³–10⁵ (a tuning value). It is learnt in 20 hours beside a knower and at home by children. Drying takes water and adds no calories (07-04): lean meat at about 70 % water dried to about 25 % keeps 0.4 kg a kilogram at 3,750 kcal/kg, and fish keeps 0.25 kg at 2,400. Both keep with half-lives of 180 days, 365 under a roof. Labour is 0.2 h/kg of meat and 0.15 of fish, plus an hour's setting up and 4 kg of wood a session, up to 30 kg. These are tuning values; the research gives no rates, and the days of hanging need no work.
- **The rotary quern as an imported route (2026-10-04, slice N, 07-04 P09; 08-03 §2.1):** it needs shaping stone and shaping wood. Grinding at a saddle quern counts 1 % of its hours toward finding it, at an E50 of 100,000 hours, so a village grinding some 6,000 hours a year would take thousands of years: in practice it comes from elsewhere, as the observer's introduction. It grinds at 0.25 h/kg against the saddle quern's 0.5 (Iron Age rotary querns, 3–5 kg an hour: 0.20–0.33 h/kg). Making one takes 40 kg of stone, 2 kg of timber and 40 hours (tuning values).
- **Tools for known work only (2026-10-04, slice N):** a household wants a tool for the members old enough for work that needs it, counting only work whose technique someone in the household knows. A tool only unknown work needs is not wanted at all, so no household wants a rotary quern until one of its members knows how to use it. Founders' kits, and tools given to saves from before schema 9, cover the techniques the people profile gives founders.

- **Slice N in the ten-year smoke (2026-10-04):** all ten worlds passed, the knowledge check included. 6 of the 10 villages kept at least 10 people: on the coast 41 and 18, and in the river valley 52, 42, 46 and 57. Coast seed 1 found drying and smoking in its sixth year, the only find in the ten worlds; its band, like river-valley seed 1's, died out, as both seed-1 bands have in every run since M1. The coast band of seed 2 fell from 45 to 7 in its seventh year, its households leaving together (the pattern of the NUDGE on founding risk), and kept 5; the coast band of seed 4 died out in its tenth year, as in slices K and L. Slice M's run kept 8. The time spent trying cannot account for it: a probe of coast seeds 1 and 2 counted every person-hour over seven years, and trying took at most 0.03 % of them (about 90 hours a year among some 45 people). One run each, and runs differ, so this does not show that slice N weakens villages; the cause of the coast seed-2 failure was not examined beyond the probe.
- **Slice O in three steps (2026-10-04, ADR-0009):** the grammar brief's B1 to B3. First the vocabulary with no change in what households do: the frame grammar, programs that say what they are for, saves and the boundary. Then a home chosen among programs, lofts sized by what a household stores, and storage as a roofed capacity. Then storehouses and workshops as second buildings. B4, loads on the groups, goes with slice P.
- **The frame grammar, as built (2026-10-04, slice O's first step, ADR-0009 §1–3):** version 1 builds a length of equal bays at a constant width under a gabled roof (research 10-06 §1.2, §2.1; 11-01 §3.4). At each frame line a post stands on each long wall and runs through every storey to the plates; a tie beam joins their heads; rafters rise in pairs from the eaves to a ridge. A loft is joists across the width on the plates over the bays the spec names, holding goods only (11-01 §2.4). An upper storey's floor and a raised floor are joists across the width on rails along the walls, with cross beams at the frame lines; a raised floor's middle rail stands on floor posts, which halves its joists' span. Walls are wattle and daub between the posts, storey by storey; the door's panel is split either side of the doorway; the gables are woven and daubed. Its sixteen parameters are ADR-0009 §2's list. Timber is member volume × 760 kg/m³ (11-08 §2.1), so a deeper joist costs real wood, and the roof is its projection over cos θ (11-04 §2.3). A spec is refused when it is off the 25 cm quantum, its length does not divide into whole bays, a value is outside the program's range, its door misses its wall, it names joists it lacks, it would need more than 32 groups (11-06 §5.1), a raised floor leaves no room for its posts, or the ladder to a raised door would stand beyond the roof's edge. Part and group ids are level, bay or frame line, kind and index, so a design with one more bay keeps every id it had (11-03 §4.8). Four goldens pin it (three bays with a one-bay loft, two storeys, a raised store, a workshop); the hut's golden is unchanged, because the hash covers the new fields only for frames, and a hut's five groups, its living space and its storage are derived beside its version-1 expansion.
- **Frame programs and their figures (2026-10-04, slice O's first step):** content API 11 has every building program say what it is for (`use`: dwelling, store or work), and a frame program's rules sit in a `[frame]` table with four material slots (timber, wattle, covering, boards). The core content has a **longhouse** (a dwelling of 1–8 bays of 2–4 m, 4–7 m wide, one or two storeys, lofts), a **granary** (a store of 1–2 bays, 2–4 m wide, its floor raised 0.5–1.2 m, after the raised, ventilated granaries of early farmers, 08-11 §2.1) and a **workshop** (a working floor of 1–4 bays with a store loft). The research gives ranges for bays, widths, storeys, eaves and pitches (10-06, 11-01, 11-05); the labour rates are the hut's where they are the same work, and a jointed frame's posts, beams and rafters are set so a three-bay house of 7.5 by 5 m takes about 1.1 pd8 a square metre of footprint for its frame, inside 11-04 §2.2's 0.5–2. That house with a loft over one bay takes about 940 hours, 2.1 t of timber and boards and 2.2 t of thatch (2.2 t is the brief's figure for its 72 m² of roof): about half as much again per square metre of living floor as a hut for five. Storage capacities are tuning values: 500 kg a square metre on a store's floor (grain about 0.65 m deep at 770 kg/m³, 11-05 §2.4), 150 in a loft, and 60 on living or working floor, which is also the hut's (`store_kg_per_m2`): room for a year's grain in a hut for five (1.26 t on about 30 m²). The second step raised living floor's figure to 100 (see storage as room, below). Places to work take 6 m² each (the research gives no figure).
- **Jointed timber framing (2026-10-04, slice O's first step, 07-07 WO02):** fitted joinery needs shaping wood and fitting skill; central European wells of 5469–5098 BCE were jointed with stone tools. The frame programs need it. Nobody knows it and nothing finds it yet (no work tries toward it, and it answers no problem), so a world has it only from the observer's introduction. It is learnt in 300 hours beside a knower and at home at building age (07-02 §2.3, a tuning value).
- **Households still build huts (2026-10-04, slice O's first step):** until households choose among programs, the people profile's home program must be a hut dwelling (a content error otherwise), and nothing a household decides reads the new vocabulary. One thing does change: a technique added to the content shifts the indexes of those after it, and finds are keyed by technique index, so finds come at other moments in a given world. A home a household holds that is a frame building (none can be, yet) counts as a round one with the same ground floor when it weighs building again, and wealth counts only dwellings' floor.
- **Saves 15, wire 1.14 (2026-10-04, slice O's first step):** saves schema 15 adds the Rect footprint (`length_cm`, `width_cm`, `angle`), sixteen parameters a design, and plot uses for stores and workshops; schema-14 saves load with their huts as they were, their other eight parameters 0. Wire 1.14 appends to `BuildingInfo` the grammar, the program's use, the size and direction, storeys, bays and loft bays, a gabled roof's corners and ridge, the apex height, floor by use, storage by kind and places to work. The observer draws a frame building's turned walls, posts and gabled roof with its ridge, and finds it under its roof rather than its circle.
- **The introduced quern's test after the founding rush (2026-10-04):** slice N's test of a rotary quern introduced on a world's first day failed in about one run in eight, since runs differ: in a world's first weeks the eldest can spend every day on fields and the first huts. That is what they would do, so the test now introduces it after 60 days, when every probe run made one within four days.
- **Homes chosen among programs, as built (2026-10-04, slice O's second step, ADR-0009 §1):** content API 12 turns the people profile's `home_program` into `programs`, the dwellings a household may build. A household builds one that someone in it knows how to build (the profile must include one its founders know). Each program's shapes are expanded once, when the content is compiled: a hut of each radius a decimetre apart, and a frame building of each count of bays and storeys, with lofts over none to all of its bays, to its usual sizes (a `[design]` table: 2.5 by 5 m bays, 15 cm posts, joists and walls, 50 cm eaves, tuning values). A first home is the shape that covers the household's members (10-06 §2.3's 6 m² plus 4.8 a resident) and its goods for the fewest hours of work, materials' carrying included. When none holds all its goods, it is the one covering its members that holds the most; when none covers its members, the one with the most living floor. Its means then buy the shape of the same program with the most floor that they pay for, as huts were sized before, so more means never give less floor. A household with a home builds again when its means pay for all of a home with at least a quarter more floor, in any program it may build. Its goods' room is what it holds now or a year's food as grain, whichever is more. A frame home's door is in the middle of the long wall that faces the hearth, its lofts from the back. Huts stay the cheaper home for five, as 11-04 §2.2's rates imply, so founders who know only huts build huts as before. The observer's introduction of a technique has its household plan its home again that day.
- **Storage as room under a roof, as built (2026-10-04, slice O's second step, ADR-0009 §5):** a household's roofed buildings give room by kind: a floor's once the roof is on, a loft's and a raised floor's once the building is finished. Goods with a sheltered half-life take the room, those that lose the most a day to the open per kilogram first (a fractional knapsack), raised floors first, then other room; the rest keep at the open rate. The shares are worked out from the stores as last settled, so spoilage is exact while the room holds everything (identical to before) and exact between settlings otherwise. The room is derived, never saved: at loading, when a roof goes on or a building is finished, when an old home comes down and when heirs take buildings, with stores settled first. Goods keep twice as long on a raised store's floor (`raised_store_factor`, a tuning value: grain's 5 % a year becomes 2.5 %, inside the 2–8 % a maintained dry store loses in 6–12 months, 08-02 §7.1). A hut's floor and a frame's living floor hold 100 kg a square metre. That is a tuning value, chosen by probing two ten-year smoke worlds (river valley seed 2 for five years, coast seed 3 for two): their households held at most 83 kg of such goods per square metre of hut floor (p90 37–70), so storage seldom binds in today's villages and M3a's calibration holds. A loft holds 150 kg/m², a store room's floor 500 (grain about 0.65 m deep).
- **Wire 1.15 (2026-10-04, slice O's second step):** `BuildingInfo` gains what its household keeps in it now, by kind of room, and in words: "loft over 1 bay: 1.2 t of 1.9 t, mostly grain". Each kind of room's goods are shared among a household's buildings by the room each gives.
- **Slice O's first step, after review (2026-10-04):** a review of the frame grammar found no blocking defects; these are its fixes. The grammar checks a floor's raise against its program's range whatever it is, so a program whose every floor is raised (the granary, 50–120 cm) refuses one on the ground; before, a raise of 0 slipped past the check. The granary's design is raised 80 cm (`[design] floor_raise_cm`, a tuning value; 08-11 §2.1), and its overhang now starts at 60 cm, so that the ladder to any raise its range allows stands under the roof. Content refuses a frame program whose bay or width could be no longer than its thickest post, and a program whose design gives nothing the grammar allows (E3001, with the grammar's reason). A household's housing counts only its dwellings: whether it is housed (and so may spend what it builds with, and stays where it lives while it builds) and how far its home is built; stores and workshops will give room for goods without housing anyone. (This step also made whether a household's stores are under a roof count dwellings only; that was wrong, since a store's roof shelters goods, and the third step undid it.) The map draws each building from a list of marks that a pure function works out (the plot, a gabled roof and its ridge or a hut's cone, the walls and posts going up, the doorway), and unit tests check them; the renderer only draws the marks. `expand_frame` is split into its layout, foundation, frame, walls, roof, finish, facts, needs and groups, with the goldens unchanged.
- **Slice O's second step in the ten-year smoke (2026-10-04):** all ten worlds passed, the knowledge check included, and every good was accounted for in every year. 7 of the 10 villages kept at least 10 people: on the coast 20, 48, 11 and 58, and in the river valley 55, 43 and 51. The bands of seed 1 fell to 3 and 0, as in every run since M1, and river-valley seed 3 fell to 3 in its fifth year and kept 4. Drying and smoking were found in three worlds: coast seed 2 (twice) and river-valley seed 2 in their seventh year, and river-valley seed 1 in its sixth. Nobody in these worlds knows jointed framing, so every home was a hut; what changed for them is storage as room under a roof, which seldom binds, and a hut sized for goods as well as members when its household holds more than its floor would. Slice N's run kept 6. One run each, and runs differ. The economy grades: food stocks and grain asks green in all ten worlds, inequality green in one and amber in nine, workshops green in three and too few to judge in seven.
- **Storehouses, as built (2026-10-04, slice O's third step, ADR-0009 §7):** content API 13 lets the people profile's `programs` name stores and workshops beside dwellings (at least one a dwelling its founders know how to build); the core profile adds the granary. A household builds one thing at a time: what it has under way (a home first), else its first home, else a storehouse if one is worth it, else a new home if its means pay for all of one (B2's rule). A storehouse is worth it when what the goods its roofs have no room for would lose in the open over a horizon, less what they would lose on its floors (raised floors keep twice as long), valued at the household's own cost of each good, exceeds the hours it takes, materials' carrying included; among the shapes its means pay for in full (as for a new home) and its time allows, the one that saves the most beyond its cost. The horizon is three years (`store_horizon_days`, a tuning value: grass thatch wants major repair every two to three years, 11-08). A granary stands within 30 m of its home, its door toward it, on a plot of its own (use `store`), and never moves its household's home; its roof is wanted by 1 September. At the core content's figures a one-bay granary takes about 530 hours and holds 3.75 t raised, two bays about 890 hours and 7.5 t; grain's own cost was about 1.6 hours a kilogram in the tests' world, so a tonne beyond the roof saves too little, eight tonnes are worth two bays, and seed kept for sowing, which is never spare, gives no means to build one. While a household builds anything, what it builds with stays for that (before, only until its home was finished). Nobody knows jointed framing in an ordinary world, so storehouses come only with the observer's introduction and play no part in the ten-year smoke.
- **A store before a larger home (2026-10-04, slice O's third step):** a hut's floor holds goods at 100 kg/m² and costs about 23 hours a square metre, so a household whose spare grain fills its hut always has the means for a home a quarter larger before it could want a store: the first version, which looked for a new home first, built longhouses where the test looked for a granary. A household losing goods for want of room now protects them first, and spends what is left on floor. The new-home rule itself is unchanged.
- **Workshops, as built (2026-10-04, slice O's third step, ADR-0009 §7):** content API 14 adds `home_work_places` to the people profile's `[build]` (two, a tuning value; the research gives no figure for how many work at a craft in a home) and the workshop to the core programs. A firm counts, whenever a session of work for it ends, how many are at work for it at that moment, owners and hired hands, and remembers the most it has seen lately (`AT_ONCE_DAYS`, 60, a tuning value: long enough that a busy season is still remembered when the household next decides what to build). A household with a home and no store worth building builds a workshop for its busiest firm that has had more at work at once than a home holds and has no workshop: the cheapest work program's building with places for them all (6 m² a place) that its means pay for in full and its time allows, beside its home with its door toward it. A firm alone never begins one. The building names its firm (`Building.firm`). Once its roof is on, the firm works there: its owners walk there to make what it sells (with the walk weighed like any other), and the hands it hires walk there instead of to the owners' home; its stock is still kept, and sold, at its owners' home. When a firm closes its building stays, and the household's next firm works in it (see the review's fixes below for how it is handed on). Heirs take every building with the firms that pass to them; a household that is no more leaves its buildings standing. Nobody knows jointed framing in an ordinary world and hired work is rare, so workshops come only with the observer's introduction and play no part in the ten-year smoke. The tests set the count by hand: a firm that had three at work at once gets a two-bay workshop of five places (about 1,000 hours); one that had two gets none.
- **Saves 16, wire 1.16 (2026-10-04, slice O's third step):** saves schema 16 adds the firm a building is the workshop of, a firm's most at work at once and the day it was seen, and each settlement's yearly roofed floor and room for goods a household; schema-15 saves load with no building naming a firm, nobody counted at work and those measures 0, and a building naming a firm the save does not have is refused. Wire 1.16 appends to `BuildingInfo` its firm and the firm's name, and to the wealth measures each household's floor under all its roofs and room for goods, and those a household in each settlement's spread. House floor area stays homes only (ADR-0007 §4).
- **Fixes from the third step's review (2026-10-04, slice O):** a review found no blocking defect and four to fix, each with a test. (1) A building under way that nobody in its household can work on (its program left the content, or nobody in it knows its technique any more) no longer holds the household up: it waits, the household plans and builds other things, and what it builds with is kept only for buildings it can work on (it had pinned every material for good). (2) A household tries its projects in order, store, then a workshop for each firm that needs one (the busiest first), then a larger home, and begins the first it can pay for and find ground for; before, it committed to one kind and gave up if that failed, and a firm busier than any workshop has places for (above ten at the core figures) shadowed every other firm for two months. That firm now gets the largest workshop its means allow. A workshop planned in the morning is begun only for a firm still open and without one. (3) A firm works only in the building linked to it. A building whose firm closed goes to the household's oldest open firm that has none, when a firm is founded and each day; before, any firm without one used it, and a new firm then took it from them. A firm that outgrows its workshop stays in it: there are no extensions yet. (4) The map's building revision counts the firm link, so the observer hears when a workshop changes hands. The smoke now counts a household as under a roof only under its own home's, and checks the new wealth measures are numbers in range.
- **Slice O's ten-year smoke (2026-10-04, after its third step):** all ten worlds pass (`smoke --years 10 --seeds 5 --size 1024`). Six of ten villages keep ten or more people (coast 3: 41, coast 5: 19; river valley 2: 56, 3: 39, 4: 46, 5: 58); coast 2 ends at 8, and coast 1, coast 4 and river valley 1 die out (coast 1 after falling to 3 in year 8, the others in years 10 and 6). Its second step kept seven, inside the six to eight of earlier slices; as worlds draw differently with every change of content, one run cannot tell the two apart. The economy checks: stocks and asks green in all ten, inequality amber in all ten, workshops green in four, amber in one and grey in five. No finds were noted. Nobody knows jointed framing in an ordinary world, so homes are huts, and the one change these worlds see is that materials are kept while any building is under way.
- **Slice P in three steps (2026-10-04, ADR-0009 §4–6):** first condition, wear and upkeep with no loads, so buildings age and are kept before anything can fail under a load; then loads, checks and failures with their chronicle; then caution per settlement and technique, with the demo test. Each step is usable and pushed on its own.
- **Building condition, as built (2026-10-04, slice P's first step, ADR-0009 §4, §6):** each component group of a building is put in place when the stage that builds it is finished, with a quality drawn once, keyed by the world, the building and the group: one less the spread at its builders' average skill times the size of a normal draw, never below 0.1. The spread runs from 0.30 for a novice (the one dispersion figure the research gives, a building-to-building log-dispersion, 11-06 §2.2) to 0.10 for a master (a tuning value), so novices' parts average 0.76 of what sound members of their sizes carry and masters' 0.92; skill buys fit and consistency, not stronger wood (11-05 §1.3, 11-08 §1.2). The builders' skill is the building skill (`core:skill/building`, content API 15) of whoever did each hour of the stage, averaged over its hours; building and mending train it (1,500 hours to 80 % of mastery, founders 0.2–0.7: tuning values, no report gives a builder's apprenticeship). Skill does not change how fast anyone builds. Quality is kept and shown, and does nothing yet: loads and margins come with the next step.
- **Wear, as built (2026-10-04, slice P's first step):** on the first of each month every group in place loses a twelfth of its kind's yearly share (each program's `[upkeep]`): a covering in the weather, infill at the wall's foot, posts at their foot by the wetness of the habitat the building stands in (the land profile's `wetness`: open water and wet margins 2, floodplain 1.3, woodland 1, upland 0.7, tuning values), and roofed timber only by how much the roof over it leaks. A group shows once its loss passes its kind's threshold and fails once it is all lost. At the core figures thatch loses 6 % a year and leaks from 15 %, so it shows after two and a half years, when the research wants significant mending (every 2–3 years, 11-08 §2.4), and is gone in about 17 untended. Posts lose 2 % a year at wetness 1 and show at 30 %; daub 5 % and shows at 20 %; roofed timber 10 % a year under a full leak (all tuning values: no report gives rates for posts in the ground or daub at a wall's foot, 11-06 §2.2, 11-08 §2.4). Decay is by exposure, never by calendar age (ADR-0009, forbidden list). A roof leaks by how far its coverings have gone beyond the share at which they leak, averaged over them, and fully once its frame or a covering has failed; the room under it for goods shrinks by that share, so what no longer fits keeps as in the open (a tuning choice: the research gives no figure for what a leak costs goods). Failed loft joists drop that loft's room. Posts that fail leave a ruin, which is no roof and no home: its household builds anew and takes the ruin down when the new roof is on; any other failure leaves the building damaged. Nothing yet fails under a load, and nobody is hurt.
- **Upkeep, as built (2026-10-04, slice P's first step):** a household mends before it builds anything new, after what it has under way: the repair under way, else the group gone furthest beyond the share at which it shows, of all its standing buildings. A repair renews what is lost of that group with that share of the group's part of its stage's labour and materials (the group's part by its members' volume, or its area for a covering or infill), and changes only that group (11-08: a new roof protects a wall without restoring what it lost; 11-06 §5.7). Its quality stays. It is weighed like building, wanted by the same roof deadline, and its materials are fetched as for building; so it is deferred while other work presses, as houses stay lived in while they show distress (11-06 §1.3). In a test a hut whose thatch had lost 60 % was mended within the season with thatch at hand: about 80 hours of thatching, begun within a week.
- **Founder skills drawn on their own (2026-10-04, slice P's first step):** a founder's skill levels are now drawn from a stream keyed by the world and the person, as their knowledge already was, so adding a skill to the content changes none of the other founding draws. The building skill first changed every band: in the seed the tests use for births, nobody arrived expecting. A skill the content has that a save never named is given to everyone on loading as a founder of their age would bring it.
- **Saves 17, wire 1.17 (2026-10-04, slice P's first step):** saves schema 17 adds each building's groups' quality, loss, state and when each was put in place and last mended, the building's state, its builders' skill hours on the stage under way, and the repair under way. Saves of schema 16 and earlier load with every group of a finished stage in place and sound as of loading, drawn as if built at middling skill (0.5), and the stage under way as worked at middling skill, so a loaded world shows no false wave of decay (ADR-0009 §8). Wire 1.17 appends to `BuildingInfo` its state, what it shows in words ("the thatch leaks; rot at the posts' foot"), the share of its roof that leaks, each group's condition and the upkeep under way in words ("mending the covering, 40% done"). The observer darkens a leaking roof and draws a ruin as bare posts.
- **Slice P's first step, ten-year smoke (2026-10-04):** all ten worlds pass. Six of ten villages keep ten or more people (coast 2: 21, 3: 42, 5: 25; river valley 2: 54, 4: 48, 5: 52); river valley 3 ends at 5 (down to 7 in year 4), and coast 1, coast 4 and river valley 1 die out. Slice O's third step also kept six. The economy checks: stocks green in nine and amber in one, asks green in all ten, inequality amber in all ten, workshops green in seven and grey in three. River valley 4 found drying and smoking in year 10. Each world ends with 9 to 13 buildings, all standing: no ruin and none damaged. The six villages that kept ten or more mended 16 to 25 parts in ten years (each counted once however often mended), and all but coast 2 (5 of 11 showing) end with nothing showing; river valley 3, down to five people, mended 8 and ends with 7 of 9 showing; in the villages that died out, every building shows its wear, with nobody left to mend it.
- **Loads and failure, as built (2026-10-04, slice P's second step, ADR-0009 §5):** each day every standing building is weighed against what it carries. Beams, joists and rafters are simply supported poles under a spread load: bending stress `W·L/8` over the section modulus `π·d³/32`, deflection `5·W·L³/(384·E·I)` (research 11-05 §5.2). Posts carry their share of the building's weight, its roof's and its lofts' and floors', against Euler's buckling load (halved for crooked poles and loose joints, a tuning value) or crushing, whichever is less. What a part carries is its covering's weight (from the grammar's materials), the goods its household keeps on it (the goods under a household's roofs, shared among its buildings by room), people on floors off the ground at 1.5 kPa (11-05 §2.4's occupied-dwelling scenario), and the month's peak of wind and snow on roofs. Timber is content (API 16, the good's `[timber]`): green oak's bending strength of 57 MPa, compression of 24.5 MPa and stiffness of 8.6 GPa (11-05 §2.3); creep φ = 1 (11-05 §2.4); and 0.6 of its strength under load carried for years (a tuning value, as the research gives no duration-of-load factor). A part's quality scales its strength, and its loss takes strength by `(1 − x)³` in bending and buckling strength by `(1 − x)⁴` (11-05 §5.1). A margin below 1 is a failure; below 1.5, or a sag past L/180, shows (tuning values). The research's L/180 is serviceability, not collapse (11-05 §2.4), so a sag is only a symptom, and failure needs a strength margin below 1 (ADR-0009 §5 read so). A loft or floor that gives way spills its goods, 30 % of them lost (a tuning value); a tie beam at the wall heads that breaks brings its lofts down; rafters that break leave the roof open to the sky; posts that give way leave a ruin. Upkeep rebuilds a part that gave way whole, but does not mend a sag or a lean, which only lighter loads cure. At the core figures, a full loft (150 kg/m²) on the longhouse's 15 cm poles over its 5 m width holds work of fair quality (a margin of about 2.7) but sags past L/180, as the brief's design note said it would; poles a novice chose badly (quality below about 0.3) give way under it; over 7 m even fair joists show the strain. A hut's twenty 12 cm posts carry its thatch and a 0.6 kPa peak about 39 times over, and give way once about three quarters rotted.
- **The month's peak load (2026-10-04, slice P's second step):** wind and snow on roofs are one draw a month for each settlement, log-normal with a median of 0.25 kPa and a spread of 0.6 (the land profile's `[peak_load]`, tuning values: wind at 20 m/s presses about 0.25 kPa and at 40 m/s about 1 kPa, 11-05 §2.4), so one month in twenty brings more than about 0.7 kPa and one in a thousand more than 1.6. It stands in for weather until M3c and will be replaced by it (ADR-0009 §5). The draw is evaluated once, never rerolled (11-06 §4); buildings were weighed each day under their month's peak, as if it came on its first day, which the step's smoke showed wrong: the storm now comes on one day of its month (see the smoke entry below).
- **NUDGE: who dies when a building gives way (2026-10-04):** the research gives no defensible share (11-06 §5.3: those on, below and beside the failed part are at risk). Each person inside, not on their way somewhere and within the building's reach, dies with a chance of 0.3 when its posts give way, 0.15 when its roof falls in and 0.05 when a loft or a floor gives way: tuning values, and the cause *collapse*. Buildings are weighed at the start of each day, when most people are at home asleep. Revisit with weather (M3c), when a storm's hour can be known.
- **Saves and the boundary for failures (2026-10-04, slice P's second step):** a person's cause of death gains *collapse* (saved as 3), and the chronicle a building that gave way (code 21): its household's eldest, those it killed, where, and in words what gave way, under what and why ("Wren's longhouse lost its loft: the joists broke under 3.8 t of provisions; they were poorly made"). Both are appended codes in fields saves already have, so the save schema stays 17 and older saves load as before. What a part shows of its load (the loft sags, the posts lean) is saved as its state and said in words on the wire; margins themselves are derived and never saved.
- **Slice P's second step, ten-year smoke, and its correction (2026-10-04):** all ten worlds passed every check, but buildings gave way far too often: 3 to 34 times a world and 245 in river valley 2, killing 1 to 15 people a world (80 deaths in all). The research's sanity band is 1e-5 to 1e-3 failures a building-year (11-06 §2.4); ten to thirteen buildings over ten years should see about one failure in ten worlds. A probe of river valley 2 found every failure in its first three years a hut's roof breaking "in a storm", four in one month. Two causes, both fixed. First, the month's peak struck every day of its month, so a roof mended after a storm met the same storm the next week: the draw now also picks the day it comes (1 to 28) and strikes only then, so each storm is evaluated once (11-06 §4, §5.2). Second, a hut's rafters are two thirds of its posts' diameter, and its 12 cm posts gave a big hut (3.4 to 3.6 m radius) 8 cm rafters over 5.5 to 5.8 m, whose margin was 1.0 at about 1 kPa when new and sound, a storm of about one month in a hundred. The hut's posts are now 15 cm and its rafters 10 cm (content: `post_diameter_cm`, `post_kg` 34, `rafter_kg` 33; tuning values, as no report sizes a hut's rafters), which carry a big hut's thatch through about 2 kPa when sound, so a roof that falls in is one poorly made or rotted under a leak. A hut for five now takes about 1.5 t of timber rather than 1 t. The step's other figures stand.
- **Caution, as built (2026-10-04, slice P's third step, ADR-0009 §6):** each settlement remembers, for each building technique, the failures of its buildings (one a building that gave way, and each death in it as two more: a tuning value) and the building-years its standing buildings of it have stood, counted on the first of each month. Both fade by half over 8 years (inside 11-09 §2.3's 2 to 20 for personal memory of a disaster, low confidence). Their ratio is the failure rate builders believe in, against at least 10 building-years so a first failure among new buildings is not taken for a rate of one in a few. Caution is `1 + (most − 1)·rate/(rate + half_rate)`, at most 2 and half way at 0.01 failures a building-year, ten times the top of 11-06 §2.4's band (tuning values; the people profile's `[build.caution]`, content API 17). A frame building is designed with its joists' diameter times the cube root of caution and its posts' times the fourth root (bending strength goes as d³, buckling as d⁴), to the nearest centimetre within the program's range, so twice the strength is 19 cm joists and 18 cm posts for the longhouse's 15 and 15. Years without failures bring caution back, since the years keep counting while the failures fade. A household chooses what to build by the usual design's cost and pays for the stronger one as it builds (the stage needs follow the design). Huts are built as always: their grammar is frozen and leaves nothing to size. A household of no settlement remembers nothing, and lessons are about a technique's buildings as a whole, not yet about one span, material or load (11-06 §5.6).
- **Saves 18, wire 1.18 (2026-10-04, slice P's third step):** saves schema 18 adds to the knowledge section what each settlement has seen of each technique's buildings: its failures and building-years and when they were last brought up to date. Saves of schema 17 and earlier load with no settlement having seen anything, so caution starts at 1. Wire 1.18 appends to `TechniqueHere` the caution there and its words ("built 1.9 times as strong: failures weigh 1.0 against 12 building-years"), shown in the knowledge panel.
- **Slice P's third step, ten-year smoke (2026-10-04, with the storm and hut-rafter corrections):** all ten worlds pass. No building gave way in any world, so nobody died of a collapse and caution stayed at 1 everywhere: inside the research's band (11-06 §2.4), where step two's smoke had 3 to 245 failures a world. Seven of ten villages keep ten or more people (coast 2: 23, 3: 42, 4: 14, 5: 56; river valley 2: 58, 4: 48, 5: 50), one more than slice P's first step; coast 1 ends at 3 (down to 6 in year 7), river valley 3 at 5 (down to 5 in year 4), and river valley 1 died out in year 6. The economy checks: stocks and asks green in all ten, inequality green in two and amber in eight, workshops green in five and grey in five. Villages mended 11 to 24 parts. Every building of every world ended showing something, where the first step's living villages ended with nothing showing: resolved in the next entry.
- **Roofs bow without showing (2026-10-04, after slice P's third step):** a probe of river valley 2 found that every building showed one thing only, its roof frame, with almost no rot: a hut's rafters under 30 kg/m² of thatch bend 1.2 to 2.5 times past L/180 over the years (creep φ = 1), so every roof "sagged" from the start and upkeep could never clear it. 11-05 §2.4 gives the L/180 to L/360 screen as a later engineering analogue for beams, not past practice, and thatched rafters bow without harm, so the sag is now checked only on lofts, floors and beams, where it hinders use. A roof frame shows only its strain (a margin below 1.5) and gives way below 1, as before. After 30 months the probe's 13 buildings show nothing. Its ten-year smoke (before deposits, which shift every world) passes in all ten worlds, and nothing gave way. Six villages keep ten or more people (coast 2: 30, 3: 13, 5: 55; river valley 2: 51, 4: 46, 5: 54); coast 1 ends at 4 and river valley 3 at 7, and coast 4 (in year 10) and river valley 1 (in year 6) die out. The four largest end with nothing showing and coast 2 and 3 with 3 of 12 and 4 of 9; the dwindled and dead bands' buildings show their wear, with too few or nobody to mend them. The economy checks: stocks and asks green in all ten, inequality amber in all ten, workshops green in four, amber in two and grey in four. Coast 2 and river valley 2 found drying and smoking in year 7.
- **Deposits as bodies, as built (2026-10-04, slice Q's first step, ADR-0010 §1):** the land profile's `[[deposit]]` rules (content API 18) lay down bodies of a good once, from the seed, on land cells whose slope and height above drainage fall in each rule's ranges: a count about `per_km2` times the qualifying land (at most 256 a rule), each at a qualifying cell drawn at random, its radius, cover, thickness and quality drawn within ranges, showing at the surface when nothing covers it or by `exposed_share`, holding its volume times `density_kg_m3`. The research describes where clay, stone and flint lie only in words (03-05 §1.1-1.2), so every figure in the core profile is a tuning value: clay on flat floodplain 0.5-6 m above a channel, 4 bodies a qualifying km² of 10-40 m radius under up to 1.5 m of cover; stone on slopes over 0.15, 2 a km², 0.1-0.6 of it fit for use (03-05 §2.2), at 2,500 kg/m³ (11-08 §2.1); flint on upland slopes, 0.5 a km², 250 kg of nodules a cubic metre of bed (no figure in the research). A new good, clay, is what clay bodies yield. A deposit is a body with a permanent id; what is taken plus what is left is always what it began with. A new world places its deposits after its founding band arrives, so the band's founding draws are as they were; later ids shift, and with them every world. Slice H's loose stone and flint per patch stay as they were. Saves schema 19 keep the bodies and what has been taken from each in a `deposits` section; a save from before it gains, on loading, the bodies a new world of its seed has (with ids of its own). Nobody finds or digs a deposit yet.
- **Finding deposits and the observer's tool, as built (2026-10-04, slice Q's first step):** when someone ends a walk, their settlement finds every body showing at the surface, with something left in it, within 50 m of the edge of the route they walked (a tuning value: the research gives no figure, and looking reveals indications rather than a body's outline, 03-05 §1.3). The settlement keeps who found it and when; the chronicle says so (code 22, appended). A buried body waits for an earthwork to cut into it; a household of no settlement keeps no record. The observer's tool lays down a body of any good on dry land, a disc of the radius asked (1 to 200 m), showing or under the cover the land profile's rule for the good gives, with the middle of that rule's thickness and quality and its density (a metre, 0.6 and 1,800 kg/m³ for a good it lays down none of); the chronicle records it (code 23). Saves schema 19 keep each settlement's finds beside the bodies. Wire 1.19 adds the deposits query and its revision in the snapshot (each deposit with its good, place, size, cover, quality, what is left and taken, and who found it in words) and the command to lay one down; the observer draws them on the map, describes them in the readout and has the tool beside *Add a family*.
- **Slice Q's first step, ten-year smoke, and a part rebuilt as new (2026-10-04):** all ten worlds pass. Each lays down 48 to 104 deposits (24 to 60 showing at the surface), and by year 10 villages have found 1 to 14 of them. Six keep ten or more people (coast 2: 15, 3: 38; river valley 2: 58, 3: 12, 4: 50, 5: 56); coast 1 ends at 2, and coast 4 and 5 (year 10) and river valley 1 (year 6) die out. Deposits shift every world's later ids, so these are other worlds than the last smoke's. The economy checks: stocks and asks green in all ten, inequality amber in all ten, workshops green in six and grey in four. But coast 2's buildings gave way 46 times, killing 6. A probe found every one a hut's roof whose rafters were poorly made, most under the thatch alone, the same roof again and again: mending kept a part's quality even when the part had given way and was rebuilt whole, so rafters too poor to carry their thatch were rebuilt just as poor. A part rebuilt after it gave way is now new members, its quality drawn afresh from its mender's building skill (keyed also by when, so each rebuilding is its own draw and no earlier draw moves); mending a worn part still keeps its quality, the new members fitted among the old. The probe's ten years of coast 2 then have 1 failure instead of 43.
- **The rebuilt part's ten-year smoke (2026-10-04):** with a part that gave way made anew, all ten worlds pass, and one building gave way in all of them (coast 2's, killing 2), where coast 2 alone had 46. Five villages keep ten or more people (coast 2: 37, 3: 41; river valley 2: 61, 4: 49, 5: 54), the least the check allows; coast 1 ends at 5, coast 5 at 8 and river valley 3 at 6, and coast 4 (in year 10) and river valley 1 (in year 6) die out. River valley 1's band has died out within ten years in every smoke since slice M but slice P's second step, which left 3, so it is not this change's doing; it wants a probe of its own. Villages mended 7 to 24 parts and found 5 to 16 of their 48 to 104 deposits. The economy checks: stocks and asks green in all ten, inequality amber in all ten, workshops green in six and grey in four.
- **Levelling plots, as built (2026-10-04, slice Q's second step, ADR-0010 §2):** a household that builds a home, store or workshop seeks level ground first: of the clear plots within the 30 m it would move a building, ring by ring outward from its home, it takes the first whose ground drops no more than `from_m` (0.4 m) across it, and when there is none the one that drops least. It never takes one that drops more than `most_m` (2 m), nor one whose platform's sides could reach a cell of water (earthworks are refused on or beside water while routing and drainage stay as generated, ADR-0010 §3). These are the people profile's `[build.levelling]` (content API 19); the research gives no threshold (11-12 §2.5B notes only that buildings often adapt to slopes instead), so both are tuning values. The ground is the generated bed, its cells' heights joined bilinearly between their centres. A plot that must be levelled gets a platform: an earthwork record with its rectangle, its level in centimetres, its sides' run (1.5 across to 1 up: 11-12 §2.3's figure for canals in ordinary earth, a tuning value for a platform's low sides), its plot and household, the earth it cuts and the share done. Its level is the one at which all it cuts, sides and all, equals all it fills, so earth is moved within the ground and never made. Volumes balance as the earth lay in the ground, as if fill were tamped back to its density in the bank; 11-12 §2.4's 0.90 compacted-to-bank prior is not yet used. The earth costs 11 hours a cubic metre cut, added to the building's first stage and done with its first hours: 8 to dig it with wooden, antler and stone tools (11-12 §2.4's prior for a non-metal kit, 1.0 m³ an 8-hour day, low confidence), 2.4 to spread and tamp it as fill (3 m³ compacted a day) and half an hour to carry it across the plot in baskets (a tuning value). 11-04 §2.2's excavation experiments dig two to four times faster, with their tools unstated. A middling hut's 6 m plot that drops a metre across it cuts 6 to 7 m³ (A·drop/8 within it, 11-12 §2.5B, and its sides), about 70 hours.
- **The ground's changes, as built (2026-10-04, slice Q's second step, ADR-0010 §3):** what earthworks have done to the ground is kept as each cell's change in mean height, in tiles of 64 × 64 cells with revisions, beside the generated bed, which is never rewritten. A platform's pure, versioned expansion (version 1) samples its plot and sides every half metre and adds each sample's change to the cell it falls in. Advancing a platform from one share done to the next adds only the difference, so no change is applied twice. A test expands the records afresh and finds the tiles, and a golden hash pins version 1. Where two platforms' sides overlap (plots keep a gap of only a metre), their changes add, each designed on the generated bed (a simplification). Walking, water and the map still read the generated ground: routing and drainage stay as generated in M3b (ADR-0010 §3), and the elevation the observer is served gains the changes with the boundary's next step.
- **Saves 20 (2026-10-04, slice Q's second step):** saves schema 20 add an `earth` section with each earthwork's record and the ground's changed tiles, each with its revision. Saves of schema 19 and earlier load with no earthworks and the ground as generated, so a building begun before then stands on its plot as it is.
- **Wire 1.20 (2026-10-04, slice Q's second step, ADR-0010 §4):** the snapshot gains an earthworks revision, which changes when an earthwork is begun or advanced or the building on its plot changes. A new query lists every earthwork with its rectangle, level, sides, earth and share done, its household, plot and building, and what it is in words ("the plot of Ada's hut, being levelled: 40% of 6.4 m³ cut and filled"), with every tile of the ground's changes and its revision. The elevation raster now serves the ground in use, the generated bed plus those changes; water, drainage and lakes stay as generated. The observer draws each platform as bare earth, more solid as more of it is done, describes it in the readout, and reads its map tiles again where a tile's revision changed. Levelling is rare: households find level ground first, and in a probe of sixteen 512-cell worlds over their first 30 days, only two coast worlds levelled any plots (one and four, all on the first day). The web shell now declares the wire minor it speaks, which had stayed at 1.16 since slice O.
- **Slice Q's second step, ten-year smoke (2026-10-04, at the levelling commit):** all ten worlds pass. Levelling is rare: coast 4 and river valley 1 levelled 2 plots each (7 m³) and coast 5 levelled 6 (15 m³); the other seven levelled none. Seeking level ground moves where every household builds, so these are other worlds than the last smoke's. Six villages keep ten or more people (coast 3: 14, 5: 57; river valley 2: 46, 3: 32, 4: 48, 5: 55), one more than the last; coast 2 ends at 4, and coast 1 (year 8), coast 4 (year 10) and river valley 1 die out. The coasts swung both ways (coast 2 from 37 to 4 and 3 from 41 to 14, coast 5 from 8 to 57), and the ten worlds hold 256 people against the last smoke's 261, so nothing here points to the change. One building gave way in all ten (coast 2's, killing 1). Villages mended 16 to 25 parts and found 4 to 17 deposits. The economy checks: stocks and asks green in all ten, inequality amber in all ten, workshops green in six and grey in four.
- **Pots as stores, as built (2026-10-04, slice Q's third step):** a good's new purpose `store` (content API 20), with `[store] keeps_kg`, is a vessel food is kept in. A household counts each store it holds as turning that many kilograms of the room in its lofts and on its floors into raised room, so the food in it keeps at the raised floor's factor. That is a tuning choice: 07-05 §3.1 lists storage among fired pottery's uses, and the research gives no keeping figure for a pot. A store stands in the room it keeps, so stores add no room and food in the open gains nothing from them. A household under a roof wants as many stores as would hold the food lying in its lofts and on its floors. It makes them by the preserving term, the food the next one would save over the store horizon (three years) valued in kcal, as long as that is at least half a day of one person's need. A household with no roof wants none. Stores are offered for sale as materials are. The core pot is a large storage pot of about 45 litres, some 8 kg of fired clay, holding 35 kg, and half are gone in three years (tuning: the research gives no size or use-life).
- **Pottery, as built (2026-10-04, slice Q's third step):** making pottery is a technique founders bring (07-05 §3.1: fired pottery among foragers 20,000 years ago, so it needs no farming before it; 06-08 §3), learnt at home, with a skill of its own taken as 1,500 hours to T80 like the others. A session forms up to five pots and fires them in an open fire in an hour (08-03 §2.1: open firings of one to three hours). Each pot is 6 hours of work (07-05 §2.3: 0.3-2 hours a kilogram of fired ware, taken at 0.75 for 8 kg), 10 kg of clay and 12 kg of firewood (0.5-3 kg of dry wood a kilogram, taken at 1.5), and one in ten breaks in the firing (08-03 §2.2's worked example). Drying between forming and firing (08-03 §2.1: a day, then three to seven more) is not kept apart: a session forms and fires its pots. A household short of the clay or firewood for the pots it wants brings it as it brings any material.
- **Digging, as built (2026-10-04, slice Q's third step, ADR-0010 §2):** a new behavior, `dig` (content API 20), names the good it `digs`, and the land profile must lay down deposits of that good. People dig at the deposit of it their settlement knows that brings most for the walk, while their household needs the good for what it builds or makes, as a material is gathered. They dig at its open pit, or where a new one would go: the free 3 m square on the body nearest the settlement's hearth, its heap beside it. An hour of a capable adult's digging brings, on average over a pit's life, the body's density times its quality times its share of the pit's depth (thickness over cover plus thickness), over `h_per_m3`. The people profile's `[digging]` gives 8 hours a cubic metre, 11-12 §2.4's non-metal prior for digging and lifting (levelling's 11 adds tamping and carrying across the plot), and 3 m pits (tuning). A household weighs a pit by that average, so it begins one under deep cover as readily as one under none, and the first sessions there bring nothing home: a 3 m pit under 1.5 m of silt is 108 hours of digging before any clay. A session digs until its load (20 kg) is full or its hours are spent. The cover and the share of the body unfit for use go on the heap; what is taken of a deposit is kilograms of its body, of which its quality is carried home. A pit is dug through its body before another is begun on it. Plots and fields keep their usual gaps from pits and spoil heaps, as from each other. The clay deposits of the core land profile hold 1,800 kg a cubic metre, so one 3 m pit through a body 0.5 m to 2 m thick yields 2.4 to 29 t of clay, many households' pots for years.
- **Finding by cutting, as built (2026-10-04, slice Q's third step, ADR-0010 §1):** a platform finds a buried body when its deepest cut, times the share done, reaches the body's cover and some of its plot lies over the body; its settlement records the builder as finder, and the chronicle says "Ash found clay while levelling a plot". The deepest cut need not lie over the body, a simplification. A pit is dug only on a body its settlement knows, so it finds nothing new.
- **Saves 21, wire 1.21 and techniques added since a save (2026-10-04, slice Q's third step):** saves schema 21 add to the `earth` section each earthwork's links to its deposit and its heap, and a person's target gains *deposit*. Saves of schema 20 load with every earthwork a platform, as they were. A technique founders bring that the content has added since a save was made is given on loading as a founder of each person's age would bring it, as a new skill is, and each settlement notes it as brought by its eldest knower; techniques the save named, known or lost, stay as they were. So a world saved before pottery loads with its potters. Wire 1.21 appends each earthwork's deposit and the kinds pit and spoil heap, which the observer draws dark and pale and describes ("Ada's household's clay pit, 1.2 m deep"; "the spoil heap of a clay pit, 3.1 m³"); someone digging is "digging clay at the clay pit north of home". The smoke notes count pits and what has been taken from deposits apart from levelled plots.
- **Pots only under a roof, and NUDGE: storage pots (2026-10-04, after slice Q's third step):** the step's first ten-year smoke was stopped at its fourth coast world. There, two of nine households went without a roof from year 2 to year 6, and the village dug 24 t of clay from one pit. Pots had been reckoned to keep anything, so a household with tonnes of grain in the open wanted a 15 kg pot for every 15 kg of it and spent its hours making them. A pot now keeps only what lies under a roof, turning room in lofts and on floors into raised room, and adds no room, so food in the open gains nothing from pots. A probe of the same world's first 30 months then found every household under a roof by its fourth month, but about a hundred 15 kg pots a household, made at 0.7 hours a day an adult in the first year. NUDGE: the core pot is now a large storage pot of 35 kg (about 45 litres, 8 kg of fired clay, 6 hours, 10 kg of clay and 12 kg of firewood, up to 5 a firing), within 07-05 §2.3's ranges. The research gives no size for a storage vessel; at this size the probe found about forty pots a household.
- **Slice Q's third step, ten-year smoke (2026-10-04, with pots under a roof):** all ten worlds pass. Five villages dug clay, in one or two pits each of 13 to 26 m³, and took 8 to 28 t of clay as it lay in the ground, before what was unfit for use went on the heaps (coast 3 and 4, river valley 1, 3 and 4). The other five found no clay they could reach and made no pots. Eight villages keep ten or more people (coast 2: 20, 3: 34, 4: 13, 5: 31; river valley 2: 56, 3: 19, 4: 49, 5: 56), two more than the last smoke. Coast 1 dies out in year 8 and river valley 1 in year 6, and the ten worlds hold 278 people against the last smoke's 256. Pots change what households do with their hours, so these are other worlds than the last smoke's. One building gave way (coast 2's, killing 2). The economy checks: stocks green in nine and amber in one, asks green in all ten, inequality amber in all ten, workshops green in six, amber in one and grey in three.
- **Daub pits, as built (2026-10-04, slice Q's fourth step, ADR-0010 §2):** the earth a building's walls are daubed with is dug from a pit beside its plot, so no material comes without a visible source (11-12 §1.1: excavation never creates free material without a borrow pit, quarry face or ditch). The daub is the walls' infill, each group's area times its thickness, read from the grammar's groups, so the frozen hut grammar and its golden hash are untouched. It is dug in step with the walls stage's work as the walls go up, and in step with the share renewed when they are mended. Building and mending cost no more hours than before: the hut's 2 hours a square metre and the frame's 13.3 hours a cubic metre were always for digging, mixing and applying daub (11-04 §2.2's cob rate). The pit is begun when first needed, on the nearest free ground to the plot, behind it from the settlement's hearth before beside or in front of it. It is a square at least the people profile's pit side (3 m), sized so that its building's daub takes it about a metre down (a tuning value): a middling hut's 3 m pit goes down about 0.6 m. All its earth goes into the walls, so the ground loses it. A pit stays when its building is gone, and plots and fields keep their gaps from it. A building from a save before this step has no pit until its walls are next mended.
- **Quarries and flint pits, as built (2026-10-04, slice Q's fourth step, ADR-0010 §2):** the land profile's deposit rules give `dig_h_per_m3`, the hours a capable adult takes to break a cubic metre of a body out of the ground, and `working`, what a working of it is called (content API 21). A body without a rate is dug as earth, at the people profile's 8 hours; its cover is always dug as earth. Stone is broken out at 12 hours a cubic metre with hammerstones, wedges and levers. At the rule's 0.1-0.6 fit for use, that is 20-120 hours a cubic metre of stone fit for use, against 11-08 §2.2's low-confidence envelope of 8-80 for small blocks from an easily worked, naturally jointed quarry. A flint bed is dug at 12 hours (tuning; no figure in the research). Two activities, quarrying stone and digging flint, dig them as clay is dug. They are weighed against gathering loose stone and flint, which brings up to 150 kg an hour where it lies thick, so people quarry only as the loose stone near a village is used up: it never renews. A test with no loose stone left sees a household that needs a quern quarry stone for it. The observer calls a stone working a quarry ("Ada's household's stone quarry, 0.8 m deep"), and someone at work there is "quarrying stone at the stone quarry west of home".
- **Slice Q's fourth step, ten-year smoke (2026-10-04, at the daub pits and quarries commit):** all ten worlds pass.
