Every village has its daub pits, 9 to 14 of them holding 38 to 72 m³ less the earth in its walls. Four villages dug clay, in one to three workings of 8 to 25 m³ (coast 4, river valley 1, 3 and 4). Four more began a working they left after 0.1 to 1.3 m³ (coast 1, 3 and 5, river valley 5): a household weighs a working by its body's average yield, cover and all, so it may begin one whose cover outlasts its need. Six villages keep ten or more people (coast 2: 31, 3: 23, 5: 34; river valley 2: 55, 4: 49, 5: 52), against eight in the last smoke. Coast 1 dies out after year 7, coast 4 in year 10 and river valley 1 in year 6, and river valley 3 ends at 4. The ten worlds hold 248 people against the last smoke's 278. Daub pits and the gaps plots and fields keep from them move where households build and farm, so these are other worlds again. The last smokes swung as widely (coast 2 from 37 to 4 to 20 to 31), and nothing in this step costs anyone an hour, so nothing here points to it. Two buildings gave way, killing 1 each (coast 2 and river valley 5). The economy checks: stocks and asks green in all ten, inequality green in one and amber in nine, workshops green in six, amber in one and grey in three.
- **Cob walls deferred, and the oven next (2026-10-04, slice Q):** the plan's clay crafts for slice Q are pottery, an oven and cob walls. Cob is a mass-wall technique, not wattle and daub (11-08 §1.1: they must not share load paths merely because both contain earth). Its walls bear the roof, are raised in lifts that dry in between (11-04 §2.2: 1.5-4 person-days of eight hours a cubic metre, drying separate), and would need a mass-wall grammar with its own structural checks. That is a grammar beside the frame grammar, not a craft, so cob walls wait for a later milestone's mass-wall grammar. The clay oven follows as slice Q's next step.
- **The clay oven, as built (2026-10-04, slice Q's fifth step):** baking in an oven is a technique founders bring (11-13 §1.2, §3.1: ovens in early farming houses at Çatalhöyük), learnt at home in 30 hours. An oven is a tool that stays where it is made (slice H's `fixed`, which was meant for it), so founders, and families the observer sends, bring none; a world loaded from a save before it gains the know-how but no oven. A household that knows how wants one for up to ten bakers. It builds it of 300 kg of clay and 30 kg of firewood with 25 hours of pottery's work (tuning values: the research gives no figure for an oven), so ovens are built only where a village knows clay it can dig. An oven lasts some 2,000 hours of baking (tuning). A firing heats it with three quarters of an hour of tending and 3 kg of wood, and up to 12 kg of flour bakes at 0.2 hours a kilogram, against the hearth's 0.39 hours and 0.5 kg of wood a kilogram. A full firing so takes half the hearth's wood a kilogram (07-06 §2.3: 5-15 % useful heat for an open fire, 15-30 % for an improved stove), and its 0.15 hours a kilogram of bread lie within 08-03 §2.2's 0.08-0.20 for a bakery's batch against 0.20-0.60 for a household's. A small batch costs more wood than the hearth would. People weigh the oven against the hearth as any two ways to the same food, and a household without an oven bakes at the hearth. A test sees founders without an oven dig clay, build one and bake in it, and the oven wear with use.
- **Slice Q's fifth step, ten-year smoke (2026-10-04, at the clay oven commit):** all ten worlds pass. Every village has its daub pits, 9 to 14 of them holding 44 to 72 m³. Five villages dug at deposits, in two to four pits and quarries of 17 to 35 m³ in all, and took 1.3 to 42 t as it lay in the ground (coast 1 and 4, river valley 1, 3 and 4). Three more began workings they left after 0.1 to 0.2 m³ (coast 3 and 5, river valley 5), as in the last smoke. Six villages keep ten or more people (coast 2: 16, 3: 13, 5: 44; river valley 2: 58, 4: 44, 5: 53), as in the last smoke. Coast 1 dies out in year 8 and river valley 1 in year 6, coast 4 is down to 3 in year 10 and river valley 3 ends at 6. The ten worlds hold 237 people against the last smoke's 248. One building gave way, killing nobody (coast 2). The economy checks: stocks and asks green in all ten, inequality amber in all ten, workshops green in four, amber in one and grey in five. The smoke does not count ovens; the next one will.
- **Style v0, as built (2026-10-04, slice R's first step, ADR-0009 §7, research 11-02):** a style trait is a decision the grammars already expand: the roof's pitch, the height of a storey to the eaves and the roof's overhang. They change how a building looks, what it costs and what it carries, so taste meets structure with no special case: taller walls buckle sooner and a steeper roof carries more covering. The four parameters frame v1 keeps for style stay 0 until a grammar expands ornament, and a hut's overhang is its program's. A household's taste is the three, held to what each program allows whenever it builds (every core roof 45-55°: 11-01 §3.2, grass thatch sheds rain from 45°). Each founding band draws a way of building around the people profile's, keyed by its settlement, and each household its own around its band's; families the observer sends together draw one keyed by the first of them, so a group joining a village brings its own. A new couple's household takes the mean of the two it came from. Once a year, after wealth is recorded, each household meets each building its settlement finished in the year past that stands whole, other than its own, oldest first, and its taste moves toward it by `alpha` × admiration ÷ `prestige_most`. Admiration runs from 1 to `prestige_most` by the mean of two ranks among the settlement's: the owner's goods among its households and the building's mean part quality among the year's new buildings. 11-02 §1.1 warns that prestige is not wealth, and names patrons and builders both, so the craft counts as much as the patron. Each building is met once, so familiarity saturates (11-02 §2.2's first safeguard), and a building that gave way, or any part of it, is admired by nobody (§5.3: a collapse weakens a design's appeal). A household remembers the building that moved its taste most, and its next building records it as what it followed: provenance (§5.2). One commission in a hundred has one trait drawn anywhere its program allows, a keyed draw; that is taste innovation, not technique (§5.4). A household chooses its building by the usual design's cost and pays for the one built to its taste, as with caution. Values: `alpha` 0.1, `prestige_most` 3 and `innovation` 1 % lie inside 11-02 §2.2's test priors (0.02-0.20, 1-3 times, 0.1-3 %, all of low confidence). The tradition (48°, 1.9 m, 0.5 m), a band's spread (2.5°, 0.1 m, 0.1 m) and a household's (1°, 3 cm, 3 cm) are tuning values. Left for later: a repertoire of whole designs rather than three numbers (§5.1), exposure beyond a household's settlement through travel, trade and marriage (M5), and admiration by reputation rather than goods and craft (notables, M4).
- **Saves 22 and content API 22 (2026-10-04, slice R's first step):** saves schema 22 adds to each household its taste and the building it admired, and to each building the one its household's taste followed. Schema-21 and earlier saves load with each household's taste drawn as its band's would be, keyed by its settlement (one band to a settlement, so families sent earlier share their village's), nobody having admired anything and no building having followed one. A test loads a schema-21 save whose tastes are wrong and finds them drawn afresh. Content API 22 adds the people profile's `[style]`. The wire is unchanged in this step.
- **A partly built oven bakes nothing (2026-10-05, after slice Q's fifth step):** an oven is built over days, 25 hours of work in sessions of one to five hours, and a tool's stock counted any of it as a worn tool still at work (down to 2 % of one), so a household baked in an oven a twelfth built. A tool that stays where it is made now works only whole. One partly built does nothing, and one worn below whole is out of use until its household mends it with more clay, which it does while it keeps the usual quarter spare. Other tools still work to the last of a worn one. A test leaves the first household with an oven begun nothing to eat but flour, and finds it bakes at the hearth until its oven is whole; before the change it failed every time. The test had passed or failed by the order of simultaneous events, which differs from run to run (each world draws its own identity). It also found that a household of two bakes at the hearth even beside its oven, as a small batch costs less wood there (the oven step's intent), so the household it watches bake in the oven is made to know no other way.
- **Wire 1.22 (2026-10-05, slice R's second step):** `BuildingInfo` appends how the building was built, in the kernel's words ("roof pitched 49°, walls 1.9 m to the eaves, after Bo's hut"; a frame building's also "eaves 0.6 m out"), and the building its household's taste followed, which may since be gone ("after a building since gone"). `PersonInfo` appends, for the living, how their household would build and the building that moved its taste most ("roofs pitched 48°, walls 1.9 m to the eaves, eaves 0.5 m out; admiring Bo's hut"). The map's readout and the inspector show them.
- **Slice R's first step, ten-year smoke (2026-10-05, at the whole-oven commit):** all ten worlds pass. Each village builds its own way: roofs pitched 46-49° in one, 49-52° in another, two or three degrees apart within a village, with eaves of 1.8 to 2.1 m. Only 9 of the 109 buildings standing at the end were built after an admired one (coast 3: 4 of 9; river valley 3: 4 of 10; river valley 5: 1 of 13; none elsewhere). After the first year, most villages build almost nothing new: river valley 2 holds 58 people in 13 buildings. The few homes begun later are mostly young couples' first homes, and a new household admired nothing until its first review; the next entry changes that. Seven villages keep ten or more people (coast 2: 15, 3: 22, 5: 25; river valley 2: 58, 3: 13, 4: 47, 5: 53), against six in the last smoke. Coast 1 ends at 4 (down to 6 in year 8) and coast 4 at 8, and river valley 1 dies out in year 6 again. The ten worlds hold 245 people against 237. Six villages dug at deposits, 14 to 34 m³ each, taking 9 to 42 t, and five hold ovens (1 to 13). Pots follow the food lying under roofs: coast 3's 22 people keep 508, river valley 3's 13 keep 237, and river valley 4's 47 keep 354. One building gave way, killing two (coast 2). The economy checks: stocks and asks green in all ten, inequality amber in all ten, workshops green in six and grey in four.
- **A new household admires what its families did (2026-10-05, slice R):** a couple's new household took the mean of its families' tastes but admired nothing, so its first home, nearly all a village builds after its first year, followed nothing until the household's first review a year on. It now also takes the building the woman's household admired, else the man's, so a young couple builds after what their families admired (11-02 §5.2: people carry a few salient exemplars with them). A unit test covers the rule.
- **Caution words for huts (2026-10-05, found in the M3b demo runs):** a village whose hut lost its roof in a storm remembered the failure, and the knowledge panel said its roundhouses were "built 1.8 times as strong". Slice P sizes only frame buildings' members, so huts were built as they always had been. A hut's grammar has nothing to size. For a technique with no frame programs the panel now says "built as usual, as a hut has nothing to size: failures weigh 0.9 against 28 building-years".
- **The M3b demo (2026-10-05):** three river-valley worlds (seeds 2, 4 and 5, bands of 40 on 768-cell maps) lived 25 years each with `civ-host run --introduce core:technique/jointed_frame`: as each world began, the observer brought jointed timber framing to the eldest grown founder of its first household, as from elsewhere. The plan's three outcomes:
  - *A craft found by one person spreads or dies with its last practitioner.* In seed 4, Vela found drying and smoking in the tenth year, the only find in the 75 village-years. It was lost that same year with Kira, the last who knew it there, as 45 people left the village. Framing passed to children growing up in its knower's household (to four knowers in seed 2 by year 15, three in seed 4, two in seed 5), but nobody practised it, so it reached nobody beyond. It died with its last knower in all three: in seed 2 in year 19, when Isla, the last of four, died; in seed 4 with the households that left in year 10; in seed 5, where its first knower had died in year 11, when the whole village left in year 14.
  - *A loaded loft sags and fails, and builders over-build for a while.* No village built a frame building, so there was no loft to load (next entry). In seed 2 a hut lost its roof in a storm in year 3, its rafters poorly made. The village remembered it, and the memory faded over the years (failures weighing 0.9 against 28 building-years, then 0.1 against 78 by year 25). A hut has nothing to size, so nobody built stronger. After villages emptied, their abandoned huts lost their roofs and fell as they rotted. Loft failure and over-building are shown by slice P's test: a loft on badly made joists gives way under a full load, and the village builds its next longhouses' joists and posts stronger for years.
  - *New houses copy an admired one.* Every one of the 16 buildings begun after the first year was built after an admired building (6 in seed 2, 8 in seed 4, 2 in seed 5), each a young couple's first home following what their families admired. The two-to-five-degree spread of roof pitches within a village stayed as it was: a village builds little after its first year, and its founders' homes stand for decades.
  - The demo's pictures come from `web/e2e/m3b-demo.spec.ts` (`TCE_DEMO=1`), one ten-year world of seed 4 with its own identity. In the run pictured it holds 49 people, and framing is known by Gorran (45) and three young people of his household, practised by none. Five of its eleven buildings were built after an admired one, two each after Gorran's and Arrel's huts and one after Tam's, and the map's readout says so: "roof pitched 50°, walls 2.0 m to the eaves, after Gorran's hut". Two earlier runs of the same command had 50 and 53 people, with five and four such buildings.
- **NUDGE: no village builds a frame (2026-10-05, the M3b demo):** a household builds the cheapest home that covers its members and goods among those it knows how to build (slice O). In the demo's villages that was always a hut, and framing was never practised: the ten-year world's knowledge panel shows it known by four and practised by none, and nobody learnt it beside a knower in 75 village-years. So framing brought to a village spreads only to children at home, and no loft is loaded or fails. Whether pots, which keep grain under a roof as a raised floor does, took away the granary's use is not established. The structural rules are tested, but a frame building appears only where a household knows framing and needs more room than a hut gives. Not nudged here: the fix belongs with what makes frame buildings worth their cost (larger households, livestock and fodder, crafts that need room), not with a push toward them.
- **NUDGE: villages of about 55 leave together (2026-10-05, the M3b demo):** all three demo villages grew from 40 to 52-59 people and then emptied within a year: 45 of 57 people left seed 2 in year 17, 45 of 59 left seed 4 in year 10, and all 54 left seed 5 in year 14. This is the pattern of *founding risk over ten years* above (asking for food evens out stores, so households run out and leave together), now in grown villages. Its cause in these runs is not established. Revisit with M3c's soils and the dashboard's fifty-year runs. (Likely cause found the same day: *Villages fail in their seed's bad years*, below.)
- **Why river valley 1 dies out (2026-10-05, a time-boxed probe):** one seven-year run of the smoke's river valley seed 1 (1024-cell map) failed as *NUDGE: founding risk over ten years* describes. Its harvests rose to 9.5 t in year 3 and then fell to 8.6 and 7.3 t while its fields grew from 40 to 50 (10 to 12.5 ha): from about 950 kg a hectare to 590. Its grain, 5.1 t after the third harvest, was gone by the sixth spring, food ran short five times, and all 44 people left within the year. Labour was not short: adults sat at the hearth five to six hours a day, and fieldwork averaged under two. What lowered the yield per hectare (the years' weather, the ground of the newer fields, sowing dates) is not established; the run report gives no field-by-field yields. (The year's climate factor for seed 1 is 0.71 in year 5: see *Villages fail in their seed's bad years*, below.) The NUDGE's remedies stand, beginning with M3c's weather and soils, which will make the cause visible.
- **Villages fail in their seed's bad years (2026-10-05, found designing M3c):** the year's climate factor, which scales every harvest and the wild plants, is drawn from `[seed, climate, year]`, an AR(1) of correlation 0.3 and spread 0.25, with nothing of the landscape in the key. So both landscapes of a seed live through the same years, in every run. Recomputed with the kernel's own draws (a check outside the repository), the low years line up with every failure the log records. Seed 1 has 0.71 in year 5 and 0.42 in year 7: its river-valley band left in year 6, and both seed-1 bands have died out in every run since M1. Seed 2 has 0.59 in year 6, the M3a demo's sixth harvest "some 40 % short", and 0.65 and 0.75 in years 15 and 16, before its demo village emptied in year 17. Seed 4 has five lean years running (0.84, 0.80, 0.86, 0.71, 0.75 in years 6 to 10), and its village emptied in year 10. Seed 5 has 0.46 in year 13, and its village emptied in year 14. The NUDGEs above on founding risk, villages of about 55 leaving together and river valley 1 now have their likely cause: each seed's fixed lean years, met with thin reserves. Fragility is still the model's: households plan on an average year and keep little more. The fix to the keying belongs to M3c's weather, which draws each landscape's own days.
- **M3c design (2026-10-05):** five slices (§7), from four briefs drawn from twelve reports, with two ADRs: [ADR-0011](decisions/0011-execution-modes.md) on the speeds and their consistency, [ADR-0012](decisions/0012-weather-and-soil.md) on weather, field water and the soil. The order: speed first (S), because fifty-year runs of five worlds take hours at today's speed (a band of 40 lives a year in about 14 s); then the dashboard (T), so a baseline is logged before anything is tuned; then weather (U), which fixes the keying that gives every seed the same lean years; then soils (V), whose harvests need the water balance; then Accelerated mode in earnest, tuning and the demo (W). Each slice is usable and pushed on its own.
- **Plan §4.4's statistical day, amended (2026-10-05, ADR-0011):** §4.4 has Accelerated mode resolve each agent's day "statistically in one daily step". It now advances a day at a time through the same events, activities and decisions as Detailed mode (01-03 reads "one statistical step per day" as one request to advance to the next day). Its approximations are declared, can be switched off in tests, and must pass the statistical test: in M3c a household view built at midnight and leisure blocks. Samplers that replace per-event machinery (company from co-location pools, crime, fire) come later, one subsystem at a time. §4.4's mode-consistency test becomes two gates: exact equivalence on every push (Gate A), and statistical equivalence nightly (Gate B), its tolerances fixed from Detailed calibration runs before any result is seen. Why: a second, statistical rule set for every behaviour from M4 to M8 would invite the divergence §8 names as risk 6, and exact work plus two approximations is expected to reach the dashboard's budget; M8's 50k agents may need the samplers.
- **The dashboard's Gini band for hoe farmers (2026-10-05, slice T):** §4.7's band of 0.3–0.75 for the Gini of wealth fits farming states. M3c's villages hoe small fields with no plough, traction or livestock and no scarcity of land, and the research puts such communities lower: 08-14 §3.1 gives 0.27 ± 0.03 for horticultural populations against 0.48 ± 0.04 for agricultural ones (Borgerhoff Mulder and colleagues, weighted across embodied, relational and material wealth, not material wealth alone), and §3.2 asks that long-lived, relatively equal farming communities be possible. So until a world has the plough, traction, livestock or scarce land, the dashboard grades the Gini of goods, the median of years 26–50 in worlds lived in by ten or more: green within 0.3–0.75, amber from 0.1 to 0.3 with this reason, red below 0.1 (no difference at all, more likely a fault in sharing than a way of life) or above 0.75. The 0.1 is a tuning value; the research gives no floor.
- **Farming earthworks move to M6 (2026-10-05, ADR-0012 §7):** M3c's contents named irrigation ditches and farm terraces. They move to M6, beside its levees and floods. Terraces and drainage help only where their constraint binds (03-04 §2.8): their value comes from soil water and erosion, which M3c begins but does not finish, and the presets' rain already exceeds emmer's need, so a ditch would have nothing to do. A ditch is also a line with a gradient and upkeep (11-12 §5.1), unlike the rectangles of ADR-0010, whose "Revisit when" item moves with them.
- **Exact speed work (2026-10-05, slice S's first step, ADR-0011 §4):** a profile of a band of 40 lived on from a save put 92 % of the time in deciding, spread thin: looking over gathering places 17 %, the settlement's travel field 15 %, pricing a purchase 12 %, the tools a household wants 11 %. The household copied for each decision, which the design's probe had measured at 34 %, no longer showed. Four changes, each exact: the population's indexes and caches hash with the engine's fast hasher; the tools an activity needs are listed without allocating, and each technique is asked about once; the search over gathering places stops once even the best return expected or seen anywhere, so far off, could not beat the best found (places come nearest first, so the same place is found sooner); and the travel field skips cells already final and, like route searches, orders its open list by one packed integer in the same order as before. From one save each (seed 2 with 40 people, 90 days; seed 4 with 125 people, 60 days), every section of the save came out byte for byte as before, at 11.1 s a simulated year against 14.9 s, and 30.9 s against 37.6 s. With 125 people route searches now take a third of the time. The same check found that a world saved, loaded and lived on parts from one lived straight through (a save rounds the paths' wear and fades it to the day saved, and the routing view is drawn again on load): Gate A's save test needs that fixed, and it is slice S's next step.
- **Saves 23: worn ground kept exactly (2026-10-05, slice S, ADR-0011 §5):** a world saved at a midnight, loaded and lived on parted within a day from the same world lived straight through. A save kept each tile of worn ground faded to the day saved and rounded to 16 bits, and loading drew the routing view and the trails again from that, while the world never saved went on planning routes on the view of the month's survey. Saves schema 23 keeps each tile exactly as it stands, at its own day, and the routing view as surveyed (a byte a cell of the tiles it covers), and the trails are traced from the view, at a survey and on load alike. Nothing else needed keeping: every other cache is a pure function of saved state (a settlement's travel field depends only on the view and the ground, which no change of a run alters, and sites and plans are found afresh each day). Gate A's tests (`civ-sim/tests/modes.rs`) now show that a world saved at a midnight, loaded and lived 29 more days ends in the same bytes as one lived straight through, and that thirty days lived at once, a day at a time or cut every 433 minutes end the same. Both bench fixtures matched as well, saved after 33 days and lived 37 more. Saves are about the same size, as the worn ground is small beside the map. Saves of schema 22 and before load as they did, their routing view drawn again. The observer's paths show the routing view, as they always did, so a world loaded within a month of its making shows no trails until its first survey; before, loading drew them. Weather and soil now take saves 24 and wire 1.24 (ADR-0012 §6 renumbered).
- **Mending waits for spring's fieldwork (2026-10-05, a test that failed one run in 24):** the test of worn daub renewed gave its household 60 days to mend walls that had lost 60 % of their daub. Run over sixty worlds with fixed identities (a new hidden hook, `Sim::create_for_tests`), the walls were mended in 32 to 66 days, most near day 50 after a 1 March start: upkeep is wanted by the roof deadline in September, so sowing and preparing fields come first, as designed (ADR-0009 §4). The four slowest passed day 60. The test now allows 120 days; the behaviour is unchanged. Gate A's tests use a fixed identity, so they live the same world every run.
- **The day step and the Accelerated speeds, as built (2026-10-05, slice S's second step, ADR-0011 §1, §2, §6):** the speeds are 1×, 3× and 10× (Detailed) and 60×, 600× and Max (Accelerated), Max an infinite speed. The mode follows the speed and is a setting, not saved state. Every advance of the kernel now stops after the boundaries of the minute it stops at (at midnight, the land's and people's day) and before that minute's events, which come first in the next advance: the order is the one an uncut advance gives, so nothing a world lives changes, and a clock stopped at a midnight stands exactly where ADR-0011 puts a switch, after the day's work and before the new day's first event. A change of mode takes effect there: at once if the clock stands at a midnight, else at the next one. At 60× and 600× the host lives a whole day once a day's time has come, never more than one a tick; switched from a Detailed speed between midnights, it first lives the rest of the day at once. At Max it lives day after day for most of each 50 ms tick, at least one. So the clock stops, publishes and saves only at midnight. Accelerated frames show nobody on a trip, and the web and C++ observers stand the clock still between them rather than carry it and the walkers on. A saved speed is kept on load (the old clamp to 10× is lifted): Max as it is, others within 1× to 600×. Gate A's tests now also show that an Accelerated clock stops only at midnight and a change of mode is held for it, that three days at 10×, ten at Max and three at 10× end where sixteen at 10× do (no approximation is declared yet), and that a world saved at an Accelerated midnight lives on as if never saved. As yet Max is only as fast as the machine: a band of 40 lives about 11 s a simulated year here.
- **Wire 1.23 (2026-10-05, slice S):** the clock carries its mode (`ClockMode`: Detailed or Accelerated), and its speed may be infinite (Max), as may `SetClock`'s; the Welcome lists the Accelerated speeds as multiples of 1× after the Detailed ones (60, 600 and infinity). Old fields keep their meaning. The observer shows the six speeds, a rule between the two groups.
- **The sanity dashboard, as built (2026-10-05, slice T, plan §4.7):** `civ-host dashboard` lives five river-valley worlds (seeds 1 to 5, the content's regimes in turn, 1024 cells) fifty years each, four at a time on four cores. Each lives its first month by the minute with the smoke's checks of it, then a day at a time at Max. Every year's end makes the checks every long run makes, now shared with the smoke (`smoke::LongRun`: nobody stuck, no population or land problems, every good accounted for, households roofed from year 2, upbringing knowledge, a first trail, claims, fields and wealth measures in order); any that fails fails the dashboard. Then the §4.7 rows that apply at M3c are graded, thresholds only: **population** (3 of 5 worlds keep 10 people at the end, and no band above max(3, 1.05^y) times its founders by the end of year y; tuning, research 05-06 §5.2 and 05-01), **food prices** (in every world with two harvests or more to judge, grain asked more than 2 log points higher before the harvest than after, and stocks rising after one at least; tuning, beside 16-01 §2.2's 17-61), **wealth Gini** (the median of calendar years 26-50 of the largest settlement while it holds 10, judged over 5 such years at least: green 0.3-0.75, amber from 0.1 as hoe farmers may be, red beyond; entry above), **firm sizes** (the mean of workshops' lifetime hours above the median where a world has 10; 16-01 §2.1) and **structural failures** (failures of buildings lived in, per 1,000 years they stood lived in, at most 2 across all worlds; tuning, 11-06 §2.4's top scenario of 1 doubled; buildings nobody lived in are counted apart, as an emptied village's huts fall). Settlement sizes, crime, epidemics and regime labels are grey with their reasons and never count as passes. It prints the worlds and the rows, writes a JSON report with `--json`, keeps each world's saves at every tenth year with `--keep-saves`, and exits with failure on any red row or failed check. Five worlds and fifty years are fixed (`--years` gives a quicker look, said to be one); a failing run is not rerun until it passes (16-03 §4.7). A nightly job runs it and keeps the report; it is too long for pull requests until Accelerated mode is faster than Detailed. The statistical consistency test (Gate B, ADR-0011 §5) comes with the first approximation, in slice W: until then a day lived at Max is exactly a day lived by the minute (Gate A).
- **The dashboard's baseline (2026-10-05, slice T, before any tuning and before weather):** the first fifty-year run (the build of slice T, with the yearly climate factor) took 44 minutes on four cores, 199 to 2,640 s a world (the last measured while other work shared the machine), and FAILED on two rows. **Population, red:** two worlds of five keep ten people at the end, both under village fields: seed 2 (59, 46, 56, 52 and 48 people at years 10 to 50) and seed 4 (45, 49, 60, 72 and 78; at most 2.0 times its founders, in year 49, within the 1.05^y bound). The household-field worlds failed: seed 1 fell to 2 in year 6 (from 44 at most), seed 5 to 5 in year 13 (from 55) and seed 3 to 9 in year 13 (from 42), living on as 4 to 11 people. Each fell in its seed's lean years (*Villages fail in their seed's bad years*, above); that both survivors are village fields is confounded with their seeds. **Structural failures, red:** 4 lived-in failures in 1,846 lived building-years, 2.17 per 1,000 against at most 2 (two in seed 3's 122 years, two in seed 4's 804); buildings nobody lived in gave way 64 times. **Food prices, green:** grain asked 3-4 log points higher before each harvest than after, stocks rising after 130 of 131 harvests. **Gini, amber:** the median Gini of goods over years 26-50 was 0.27 in seed 2 and 0.33 in seed 4. **Firm sizes, green:** the mean of workshops' lifetime hours 1.2-1.9 times the median where a world had ten or more (seeds 2, 4 and 5). Settlement sizes, crime, epidemics and regimes grey. Nothing was tuned against it: weather (slice U) changes which years are lean, and soils (V) and the household view (W) come before any tuning.
- **Weather, as built (2026-10-05, slice U's first step, ADR-0012 §1, §2, §5):** `civ-land`'s `weather` module draws one daily series for each world from `[seed, weather, landscape, day]`, the landscape being its preset's id, the same at every speed. **Rain:** a two-state chain, its wet days by month (0.36-0.43 of days with 1 mm or more; research 03-03 §2.2 gives Cfb and Dfb 0.25-0.60 in the wet season, 0.08-0.45 in the dry) with persistence 0.3 (0.1-0.5); a wet day's amount 1 mm plus a gamma draw of shape 0.8 (0.5-2.0), each month's mean its share of the preset's annual precipitation (a summer maximum, as inland; tuning). **Temperature:** monthly normals at 150 m (January −1 °C, July 19 °C; tuning) plus an AR(1) anomaly of 0.7 (0.4-0.8) and 2.5-4 °C by month (Cfb 2-4, Dfb 3-6); a wet summer day 1 °C cooler than a dry one and every wet day's range narrower, each month's mean kept; 6.5 °C less for each km of height (§2.1). **The slow anomaly**, a monthly AR(1) of 12 months (§2.2: 6-36), scales wet-day amounts by exp(0.1s − 0.005), the share of wet days by 1 + 0.1s and May to September by −0.8 °C a unit, so wet and dry spells cross seasons with the mean kept (§1.6). **Snow** falls below a daily mean of 0.5 °C, lies in bands of 100 m and melts 3 mm of water a degree-day (tuning). **Soil water:** reference evapotranspiration from temperature (Hargreaves, FAO-56, at the people profile's latitude); the root zone holds 100 mm, drawn unstressed until 55 % is used (FAO-56's p; slice V's soils will set these by ground). The soil under the wild cover (crop coefficient 0.9) is the reference; each growing field keeps its own balance from the day it is sown to the day it ripens, starting from the reference's water that morning, with emmer's coefficients 0.4, 1.15 and 0.4 over stages of 30, 30, 40 and 20 days (03-02 §2.1 gives maize's 0.40 and 1.15; tuning). **The harvest** scales by 1 − 1.15 × (1 − water had ÷ needed) (08-02 §7.2, spring wheat), divided by that figure's long-run mean for the landscape, drawn over 300 years from the landscape's own stream when a world is made or loaded and never saved: 0.76 in the valley, so a season with all the water emmer needs gives 1.3 times the content's yield. **Wild plant food** grows by the cover's ease of drawing water, smoothed over a month, against what the month usually has; fallen wood, poles and reeds grow as authored. The yearly climate factor and its two parameters are retired. Saves schema 24 (an older save's weather starts from the old year's deviate as its slow anomaly, with the soil at field capacity, no snow, and a crop growing then unstressed so far); content API 23 (`[weather]`, `follows_water`, a crop's `kc`, `kc_days` and `ky`). **Measured:** 500 years of the valley give 803 mm a year (CV 0.22) on 139 wet days, a mean of 9.2 °C, 101 frost days and 37 days with snow lying on the valley floor, and a harvest factor of mean 1.01 and CV 0.22 (08-02 §7.3: 0.20-0.35 for rainfed farming in a favourable climate, all causes together), below 0.8 in 21 % of years and below 0.6 in 3.6 % (the retired factor: 21 % and 5.5 %). The coast's 1,019 mm give a harvest CV of 0.18. A year's rain correlates with the year before's at 0.32 (0.37 on the coast), more than most stations show (the slow anomaly; tuning), and a harvest with the one before at 0.12 (the retired factor: 0.3). The first fifty years of seeds 1 to 5 average 747-879 mm and harvest factors of 0.93-1.06; seed 1's first twelve years are dry (728 mm, a factor of 0.89). `civ-host weather --seed N` replays a world's weather on its valley floor without making the world, and `civ-host run` reports each year's with the water its crops had. Slice U is complete.
- **NUDGE: late sowing is counted twice in part (2026-10-05, slice U):** emmer still loses its authored 0.5 % a day of sowing after 21 March, a tuning value that stood for everything a later season costs. A later crop now also meets the drier weeks of July and August in its water balance. How much of the authored loss the water now carries is not measured; revisit in slice W's tuning, not before. *Measured the same day:* the water balance alone gives emmer sown on 5 May 6.2 % less than emmer sown on 21 March in the valley, and 3.3 % less on the coast, against the authored 22.5 %: about a quarter and a seventh of the authored loss is counted twice.
- **Wire 1.24 (2026-10-05, slice U):** the clock carries today's weather on the valley floor (`DayWeather`: rain and snow, mean, lowest and highest temperature, snow lying, the soil water under the wild cover, and the kernel's words: "9 °C, rain on dry ground"); the snapshot a `weather_rev` that changes each day lived; `GetWeather` answers a `WeatherReport` of every month lived (its rain and the month's long-run mean, wet days, mean temperature and its normal, lowest and highest, frost days, days with snow lying, and mean soil water) with today's weather; and a field says the share of its water need its growing crop has had (`water_had`, −1 before it has needed any) and its root zone's water (`soil_water`). Old fields keep their meanings. The observer shows the words on the clock and a weather panel of the last twelve months and each calendar year against the usual; the field readout's status says how much of the water it needed a crop has had.
- **Slice U's first step, ten-year smoke (2026-10-05, at the weather commit):** nine of ten worlds pass, in 14.5 minutes. Coast 4 fails the roof check: two of its nine households lived without a roof from year 2 to the end, with 47 people at year 10 (next entry). Coast 5 dies out (down to 3 in year 3) and river valley 1 ends with 4 (down to 3 in year 5); the others keep 11 to 56 people (coast 1: 20, 2: 26, 3: 11; river valley 2: 56, 3: 11, 4: 45, 5: 43), 263 in the ten worlds against 245 in slice R's smoke, under other weather. One building gave way, killing nobody (coast 2). The economy checks: stocks green in all ten; asks green in nine and grey in one; inequality green in one, amber in eight and grey in one; workshops green in six and grey in four.
- **A worn-out axe could not be replaced (2026-10-05, probing coast 4):** a probe of coast 4's first year under a fixed identity found the two roofless households' huts at their first stage from days 92 and 99, waiting for poles. Each household held 2 % of an axe, no timber and 0.4 kg of flint; its adults left cutting poles out for want of a tool, and building for want of materials. An axe needs a kilogram of timber for its haft, and timber came only from cutting poles, which needs an axe: a household whose axe wore out with no timber in store could never make another, and nothing in the village broke the loop. The core pack adds `core:activity/cut_rods`: thin rods and saplings cut with a flint flake or broken by hand, at a fifth of an axe's rate (research 07-05 §1.3: a usable cutting flake needs little finishing; 03-06 §2.5: thin rods as well as poles; the rate is a tuning value, as the research gives none). The kernel's rule that leaves slower field work out while a tool for faster work is free now covers gathering, more strictly: a slower way to gather a resource is left out while the household holds a tool for a faster one, even in another member's hands. Cutting by hand whenever the household's axe was busy took hours from other work (two fewer fields in one probe's first season) and held back a household's first hut in a test, so it is kept to households without an axe; reaping by hand beside a busy sickle stays, as a ripe crop cannot wait. Under the same two identities coast 4 then roofs all nine households in its first year: one lives exactly as before, as none of its households ever lacked an axe, and the other harvests 2 to 4 % more over three years (4.2, 7.4 and 12.5 t against 4.1, 7.3 and 12.3) with the same people.
- **Workable days and the farmers' view (2026-10-05, slice U's second step, ADR-0012 §4, §5):** work that turns the soil, breaking and preparing ground and sowing, waits for a day the ground can be worked (as first built, all field work out of doors waited: below). Snow lying at a field's height (5 mm of water or more), 5 mm or more of rain or snow in the day (the weather profile's `wet_ground_mm`, a tuning value) or a daily mean below freezing at its height (`frozen_below_c`) keeps people from it, and their choices say why ("the ground too wet to work", "snow on the ground", "the ground frozen"); weeding, reaping and threshing go on whatever the weather. From its 300 years the climatology measures, for each crop, the share of the days of its window for preparing and sowing that can usually be worked on the valley floor: 81 % in the valley and 76 % on the coast. Households plan their spring work on those days (next entry), and the urgency of preparing and sowing counts only the days left that can be worked, so the work presses harder. A growing crop is judged by the water its season so far has brought (§4: people see the season so far, never future draws): its expected harvest, by which tending and reaping are weighed and which the field readout shows, is an average year's for the rest of the season and what its water so far allows for the share of a season's usual need already passed (417 mm in the valley, 414 on the coast). `civ-host weather` prints each year's workable days of the window. Content API 24 (`wet_ground_mm`, `frozen_below_c`, `peak_work_hours_per_day`). **Measured:** a probe of coast 4's first two years by the hour, under a fixed identity, found a fifth less preparing in March and a fifth more in April, and the season's hours of preparing and sowing within 1 % of before; the first harvest was 4 % smaller with the same 25 fields, and the second 10 % smaller (43 fields against 45), sown later and reaped over more wet days. Over four years under two identities coast 4 harvested 3.9-4.0, 6.5-6.6, 10.1-10.3 and 11.0-11.3 t (before: 4.1-4.2, 7.3-7.4, 12.3-12.5 and 12.1-12.7) and kept 37 to 45 people (41 to 44). River valley 1 harvested 3.4-3.6, 7.4-7.9 and 6.8-6.9 t (3.7-3.8, 8.2-8.5 and 7.3), 3 to 13 % less, from fewer hectares under one identity and smaller yields under the other, and in its fourth year fell to 6 and 11 people where before it kept 44 and 42: it was already the most fragile world, down to 3 in year 5 in the last smoke.
- **NUDGE: spring plans count on peak hours (2026-10-05, slice U):** planning spring work on the days that can be worked at ordinary hours (6 a day) shrank the area a household would take on by the days lost, about a fifth. In that first build coast 4's first two harvests fell by 14 to 23 %, and under both identities probed its households left the valley in year 3, 6 people of 44 staying, where before it kept 41 to 44. Research 04-02 §2.4 gives 10 hours of field work at a peak against 6 ordinarily, so a household now plans on the days that can usually be worked at peak hours, never on more than every day's ordinary work: while three days in five or more can be worked, the plan is what it was, and the weather takes only the days themselves. The people profile's `peak_work_hours_per_day` carries the figure. Whether people really give the longer hours on the days they can work is not measured: the probe found the season's hours of preparing and sowing unchanged, only later.
- **Workable days narrowed to the work that turns the soil (2026-10-05, slice U):** the ten-year smoke at the workable-days commit FAILED. River valley 4 sowed no field in its first 30 days (11 marked out): snow lay on its valley floor on 65 days of its first year, and only 42 of that year's 67 days of the window for preparing and sowing could be worked. Only 3 of the 9 bands that lived on kept ten people (coast 2: 28, coast 4: 45, river valley 2: 60), against 8 of 10 in the weather commit's smoke: coast 3, coast 5 and river valley 3 died out, and coast 1, river valley 1 and river valley 5 ended with 6 to 8 people. CI's month-long smoke failed on river valley 4 too. A probe of the four worlds that fell (coast 1 and 3, river valley 3 and 5, one identity, six years) against the build before workable days found holding back weeding and reaping on wet days did most of the harm (coast 1: 6 people in year 6, against 21; river valley 5: 3, against 41). Judging crops by their water so far made no consistent difference. Weeding goes on in wet weather in practice, and the model has no wet grain or drying to make a wet day at harvest cost more than the time lost, so only breaking and preparing ground and sowing now wait for the ground. Even so, workable days cost the most fragile worlds: in the probe coast 3 left in year 4 (34 people at year 6 before) and river valley 5 in year 5 (41 before), coast 1 kept 20 (21), and river valley 3 fell in year 6 either way. Without workable days these worlds are already marginal: coast 1 halved in year 5 and river valley 3 fell in year 6. The smoke's first-month check now asks for a sown field only when the ground could be worked on at least 15 of its first 30 days, 3 of them in the sowing window (`smoke::WORKABLE_DAYS`): river valley 4 could work its ground on 11, the other worlds on 18 to 29. Survival is the dashboard's to answer: households plan their fields on the content's yield, not on what their fields have given, which slice V's field records change, and slice W tunes.
- **Slice U's second step, ten-year smoke (2026-10-05, with workable days narrowed to the work that turns the soil):** all ten worlds pass, and at the edge: 5 of 10 bands keep ten people (coast 1: 12, coast 2: 32, coast 4: 47; river valley 2: 59, river valley 4: 49), the fewest the check allows. Coast 3, coast 5, river valley 3 and river valley 5 die out (in years 4, 3, 6 and 5) and river valley 1 ends with 3: 202 people in the ten worlds against 263 before workable days. River valley 4's first month, with 11 days the ground could be worked, no longer fails it. One building gave way, killing two (coast 2). The economy checks: stocks green in nine and grey in one; asks green in seven and grey in three; inequality green in one, amber in six and grey in three; workshops green in two and grey in eight. 11.7 minutes, while other work shared the machine.
- **Wear by wetness (2026-10-05, slice U, ADR-0012 §5):** on the first of each month thatch, daub and the feet of posts wear as wet as the month just lived was against its usual on the valley floor: a month with twice its usual rain and snow wears them twice as fast, and one with half of it half as fast, from a quarter (a month without rain still wears, in sun and wind) to three times (tuning values). Rafters and joists still rot only as the roof over them leaks. Over 300 years of the valley a month brings 1.01 of its usual on average, more than twice it in 3.0 % of months and less than a quarter in 1.5 % (the coast: 0.99, 2.8 % and 2.4 %), so the authored rates hold on average, and a year's wear runs from 0.59 to 1.65 of an average year's (standard deviation 0.20; the coast 0.51 to 1.71). No content or saves change.
- **Wire 1.25 (2026-10-07, slice U):** today's weather (`DayWeather`) appends the snow line, the lowest height at which snow lies: the foot of the lowest 100 m band with 5 mm of water or more lying, and infinite when it lies nowhere (as an older host's weather reads). The observer shades the map's land for the season the clock tells. It dulls toward grey-brown from September to April, most in December and January; from April to October it yellows as the soil water under the wild cover on the valley floor falls below 60 % of what the soil holds; and above the snow line it is white. The tints are the observer's, for showing only: the kernel gives the month, the soil water and the snow line. The map shades its terrain again only when the month, the soil water by tenths or the snow line by 50 m changes, at most every two seconds. Water, fields, worn ground and buildings are drawn as before.
- **The weather in the chronicle, saves 25 (2026-10-07, slice U, ADR-0012):** on the first of each month the chronicle notes the month just lived if its weather on the valley floor stood out against what it usually brings: rain and snow at twice the month's long-run mean or more (wet) or at a quarter of it or less (dry), or a mean temperature 3 °C or more below its normal (cold) or above it (warm), in the kernel's words ("October was wet and cold on the valley floor: 168 mm fell, 2.1 times what October usually brings; a mean of 3.5 °C, 6.0 °C below its usual."). On the first of May it notes the winter from October if snow lay on the valley floor on 1.75 times the days a winter usually has or more (long), or on a quarter of them or fewer (mild), and at least 10 days from the usual either way, so a landscape where snow seldom lies has neither mild winters nor long ones of a few days. On the first of January it notes the calendar year if its rain and snow came to 1.3 times its usual or more (wet) or to 0.7 of it or less (dry). The climatology also measures the usual days of snow lying by month (derived, never saved). The thresholds are tuning values, chosen so that what is noted stands out: over 500 years of the valley, 543 months were noted (9 %: 193 wet, 107 dry, 102 cold, 154 warm), 69 winters (14 %: 48 long, 21 mild) and 83 years (17 %: 51 wet, 32 dry), about 1.4 entries a year. Snow by the month was measured and left out: ten days more of snow lying than usual marked a fifth of Januaries, as in deep winter snow lies all month or hardly at all, so a winter is judged whole. The entries say what the weather was, never what it did (research 15-02 §1.2 keeps observation apart from explanation), and they come whoever lives in the world. Saves schema 25 adds the chronicle's code 24; saves of schema 24 load as they are.
- **Storms and snow on roofs (2026-10-07, slice U, ADR-0012 §5):** roofs carry the weather, and the monthly draw for each settlement that stood in for it is retired. **Storms:** once a month a storm crosses the world's landscape, one draw from the weather's own key (`[seed, weather, landscape]`) for each month, on one day of it (1 to 28), the same for every roof under it. Its gusts load a roof log-normally about 0.25 kPa on its plan with a spread of 0.6, the stand-in's figures, now wind alone: one month in twenty above about 0.7 kPa, one in a thousand above about 1.6 (research 11-05 §2.4: q = ρv²/2, about 0.25 kPa at 20 m/s and 1 kPa at 40 m/s; tuning values). They are the same in every month, as the research gives no season of storms. **Snow:** a roof carries the snow lying on the ground at its building's height, its water's weight (11-05 §2.4: p = ρgd from the snow lying), of which a roof keeps 0.8 when pitched at 30° or less, falling to none at 60° (tuning values: the research gives no share): a hut's 45-55° thatch keeps 0.13 to 0.4 of it. Over 500 years the year's deepest snow on the valley floor holds a median 33 mm of water, 68 mm one year in ten and 108 mm one in a hundred (1.06 kPa on the ground); at 350 m, 46, 91 and 142 mm. So a hut's roof carries about 0.1 kPa of snow in an ordinary winter and up to about 0.4 in a hard one, beside the month's storm on its day. A building that gives way under more snow than storm, with 0.1 kPa or more of snow on its roof, is told of as giving way "under snow". Content API 25 moves the figures into the weather profile (`storm_median_kpa`, `storm_spread`, `roof_snow_share`, `roof_snow_full_deg`, `roof_snow_shed_deg`) and retires `[peak_load]`; no saves or wire change (the storm is a keyed draw; the snow lying is saved already). **Ten-year smoke** (9.9 minutes): all ten worlds pass, at the edge as before: 5 of 10 bands keep ten people (coast 1: 42, coast 2: 25, coast 4: 48; river valley 2: 59, river valley 4: 46), coast 3, coast 5 and river valley 5 die out and river valleys 1 and 3 end with 3 and 4: 227 people in the ten worlds against 202 in the last smoke, under other draws of who the founders are. Two buildings gave way, killing nobody (coast 2, river valley 2), against one in the last smoke: no sign that the failure band moved, which the next dashboard run measures. The economy checks: stocks green in all ten; asks green in seven and grey in three; inequality green in one, amber in six and grey in three; workshops green in three and grey in seven.
- **Grain asks that answer stores (2026-10-07, slice U's last step, ADR-0006 §4's stock term):** a household's ask for food answers what it holds beyond its needs. Its anchor, its own cost and margin, is multiplied by exp(−0.5 × s), `s` the years of its own need it can spare, at most one, so a household with a year's food to spare asks 0.61 of its anchor (research 08-04 §1.2: a cost anchor with modest inventory feedback; the 0.5 is a tuning value, as the research gives none). The spare is measured in years of need rather than against the store kept to the next harvest, so a store drawn down as planned does not move the ask (08-05 §1.5 warns of that artifact); the ask still moves at most 5 % a review. Content API 26 (`market.stock_response`). **Probe:** river valley 2 under village fields, two fixed identities, eight years, with the term and without. Without it the median grain ask stood at 1.845 hours a kg after every harvest and 1.90 before, whatever the harvest (99 to 339 kg a head). With it, asks after the harvest fell as good harvests (308 to 339 kg a head) filled the stores, from 1.76 and 1.79 in year 1 to 1.12 by year 7, and under one identity rose again after the leaner harvests of years 4 (174 kg a head: 1.46 to 1.53) and 8 (249: 1.12 to 1.21); under the other they held after years 4 and 8 (186 and 233 kg a head), its sellers still holding a year or more to spare. Harvests and head counts were the same under both, year by year: in eight years the asks changed nothing about who ate. **Ten-year smoke** (10.5 minutes): all ten worlds pass. Grain is asked 6 to 16 log points more before the harvest than after in the seven worlds with enough harvests to judge, and 47 in one over two harvests, against 3 to 4 before (research 16-01 §2.2: 17-61 in markets that also carry credit and risk). Populations as before, at the edge: 5 of 10 bands keep ten people (coast 1: 21, coast 2: 29, coast 4: 48; river valley 2: 61, river valley 4: 47), coast 3, coast 5 and river valley 1 die out, and river valleys 3 and 5 end with 4 and 5: 215 people in the ten worlds. Two buildings gave way, one killing one person (coast 2). The economy checks: stocks green in nine and grey in one; asks green in eight and grey in two; inequality green in two, amber in six and grey in two; workshops green in five and grey in five. With this step slice U is complete.
- **The soil, as built (2026-10-07, slice V's first step, ADR-0012 §3):** each field keeps its soil's nitrogen in two pools, kilograms a hectare: humus turning over at 0.02 a year and roots, stubble and fresh organic matter at 0.3, RothC's base rates for humified and resistant plant matter (research 03-04 §2.5). Native ground of average richness holds 2,600 kg in humus (03-04 §2.1 works 0.2 m of soil at 1.3 t/m³, 1 % organic carbon and a C:N of 10 to 2.6 t) and 100 kg in the fast pool (tuning), both scaled by the field's ground. On the first of each January, with no draws, each pool releases its share, which with 15 kg from the air and free-living microbes (tuning: the research gives no pre-industrial figure) is the year's supply, and last year's unused supply is lost: native ground releases 82 kg a year (03-04 §2.1: 39-91). A crop takes up 0.6 of the supply (03-04 §4.2: 51-68 % of fertilizer nitrogen recovered with enough P and K) and emmer 0.035 kg for each kilogram of grain it grows (tuning: grain 0.020 and straw 0.006 a kilogram, 03-04 §2.2, with 1.5-2 kg of straw a kilogram of grain and the roots), so native ground allows about 1,660 kg/ha, above what any season gives. A harvest is the least of what its season and its soil allow (03-04 §5.2). The grain and the straw carried home take their nitrogen off the field, and the rest of what the crop took up, its stubble and roots, returns to the fast pool. A field that gave no harvest the year before grew its wild cover, which brings each pool back toward native ground's at the pool's own rate: nitrogen is never made, and cropping every year runs a field down over decades toward what the air's nitrogen keeps, about 260 kg/ha, never to nothing (03-04 §4.1; in a scratch model of one field, harvests in the fifth decade were about 80 % of the first decade's and in the tenth 53 %, while two crops and a rest year held about 90 %). Each field remembers its last eight harvests per hectare and which of the two held each back. Saves schema 26 (an older save's fields are cropped from native ground of their richness for as many years as each has given harvests, with none on record); content API 27 (`[soil]`, a crop's `grain_n_kg_per_kg`, `straw_n_kg_per_kg` and `crop_n_kg_per_kg`). `civ-host run` reports each year's harvests per hectare, how many the soil held back and the soil's supply against native ground's. **Ten-year smoke** (8.4 minutes): FAILED by one band. Four of the nine bands that lived on kept ten people (coast 2: 20, coast 4: 47; river valley 2: 60, river valley 4: 49), coast 5 died out and coast 1, coast 3 and river valleys 3 and 5 ended with 7, 4, 4 and 3, and river valley 1 failed its first month: on day 30, 5 of its 8 households had no firewood. Neither is the soil's. Nothing in the first month touches it (no harvest, no turn), and river valley 1's first month passed in eight more runs of this build under fresh identities. Ten years of coast 1 and river valley 5 (768-cell maps) gave 0 harvests held back by the soil in the 4 and 6 years they cropped, the soil's supply falling to 87 % and 78 % of native ground's; both bands left (coast 1 in years 3 to 5 with harvests of 637 to 981 kg/ha, river valley 5's 47 people at once in year 7 after a 600 kg/ha year). Coast 1 has ended between 6 and 42 people in the smokes of slice U. The economy checks: stocks green in eight and grey in one, asks green in eight and grey in one, inequality amber in eight and grey in one, workshops green in four and grey in five. Survival stays at the edge, as the dashboard has measured since its baseline; slice V's next step lets households plan from what their fields have given.
- **Planning from the fields (2026-10-07, slice V's second step, ADR-0012 §4):** households plan from what they can see, never the soil. A field is expected to give the mean of its last three harvests a hectare within five years, or with none, what the crop gives on ground of its quality (tuning values). A household's need is grain, not area: the grain its members eat in a year with what is lost before it is eaten. It crops the fields already begun, then the others best first, by the grain each is expected to give beyond its seed (at the cautious share it plans on) for the work it needs before it is sown, until they are expected to grow that need; the rest rest (research 08-02 §6: the land a household holds is not the land it sows). It marks out new ground only when all its fields cropped would fall short. New ground, and ground grown over, can be broken on any day the ground can be worked, out of the season too: broken out of it, it lies broken until the window, when it is dug like cropped ground. A household breaks ground out of season when it has the seed for everything it means to crop, can break the new ground before the window opens and can dig and sow it all in the window; nothing presses on that work until the window. Broken ground left unsown three whole years has grown over and is broken again (not cleared again) before it is cropped (content API 28, `[soil] regrown_years`; a tuning value). The communal allocation of village fields and the seed set aside plan at the yield each household's fields have given. The observer calls such ground "grown over, to be broken again", and `civ-host run` counts it. **Ten-year smoke** (9.6 minutes): all ten worlds pass, and all ten bands keep ten people (in the later smokes of slice U, five did): coast 1: 51, coast 2: 48, coast 3: 49, coast 4: 48, coast 5: 17; river valley 1: 52, 2: 55, 3: 48, 4: 46, 5: 54, 468 people in the ten worlds against 215 to 227 in the smokes of slice U. Breaking ground in the autumn takes its 1,000 hours a hectare out of the spring, which had bound the area sown since the workable days; households now hold and crop much more ground. In ten years of river valley 5 (768-cell map) its households held 68 fields at the end of year 1, 27 of them reaped that year, and 115 in year 10, 99 reaped (about 0.48 ha a person, 751 kg/ha): grain 20 to 35 % above their need in an average year, the buffer that planning on a cautious share of what fields gave implies (10-01 §2.3). The soil now begins to tell: its supply fell to 77 % of native ground's by year 10, and in year 9 it held back 4 harvests. Coast 1's households, 37 people after 16 left in year 2, held 69 fields by year 7, reaping 57 to 62 of them, and let 1 to 3 grow over. Three buildings gave way, each killing one person (coast 1, 2 and 3), against one or two in each smoke of slice U, as larger villages store more: the dashboard measures their rate. The economy checks: stocks and asks green in all ten, inequality amber in all ten, workshops green in eight and amber in two. Survival is no longer at the edge in ten years; the fifty-year dashboard has not yet been run with soils, and slice W's tuning answers to it.
- **Middens and manuring (2026-10-07, slice V's last step):** each household keeps a midden beside its home, settled on the first of each month and whenever it is drawn on: each member adds 0.5 kg a day of ash, food waste, sweepings and dung holding 1 kg of nitrogen a year, and the heap loses half of itself and its nitrogen to the air and the rain in a year (tuning values; research 12-02 §2.3: a person's faeces hold 0.42 kg a year and their urine 3.56, most of which soaks away; 12-02 §3: early farming middens mixed domestic waste, ash and fecal material). A test holds the content to a share below the 4 to 5 kg of nitrogen in a year of the grain a person eats, so a midden returns part of what harvests took and never makes nitrogen (03-04 §5.5). Founders bring `core:technique/manuring` (07-04 §4.4: documented in Neolithic Europe by the sixth millennium BCE), which gates `core:activity/manure_field` and the new field task `manure`. A household carries dung, in 25 kg loads there and back with 2 hours a tonne to dig out and spread it (research 08-12: a porter carries 15-30 kg; tuning), to a field it means to crop that lies broken or dug and has given less than new ground of its kind, the one where an hour brings most first, so near fields are dunged before far ones (03-04 §2.3: intensive manuring is concentrated near settlements). People value it by what dung is known to bring the next harvest, never by the soil: its nitrogen, the tenth the year's crop draws on, taken up at 0.6 and over the crop's 0.035 kg a kilogram of grain, up to the field's shortfall from new ground's. The dung enters the pools, 0.3 of it the fast one (content API 29: `[soil] manure_fast_share`), so a tenth of its nitrogen reaches the year's crop wherever in the year it is spread (03-04 §2.3: 5-15 %) and the rest feeds later years. In the first build households took up dung as soon as the heap held a load, while a session runs an hour at least: about 0.1 hours an adult a day went to carrying, ten times what the heaps held. Manuring now waits until the heap holds an hour's carrying. Saves schema 27 (a household's midden; older saves begin it empty on loading); content API 29 (`[midden]`, `manure_fast_share`, the technique and the activity). The observer tells each field's last harvest and whether its soil held it back ("fallow; last gave 512 kg/ha in year 7, held back by its soil"), and `civ-host run` what the middens hold. **Ten-year smoke** (17.8 minutes, beside the dashboard): all ten worlds pass and all ten bands keep ten people (coast 1: 54, 2: 27, 3: 54, 4: 50, 5: 17; river valley 1: 54, 2: 58, 3: 48, 4: 41, 5: 57; 460 people); one building gave way, killing nobody; stocks and asks green in all ten, inequality amber in all ten, workshops green in nine and grey in one. In ten years of river valley 5 (768 cells) its middens held 0.3 to 0.8 t at each year's end against the 11 to 12 t they would hold if none were carried out: households carry out nearly all their dung, about 8 t a year, which at about 2 kg of nitrogen a hectare a year over the 25 ha they crop leaves the soil's drawdown as it was (its supply at 77 % of native ground's by year 10). At hand scale and village size a midden matters on the fields it is carried to, not across the village's ground, as research 03-04 §2.3 leads one to expect. With this step slice V is complete.
- **The dashboard with soils (2026-10-07, after slice V's second step, `269edf3`):** the fifty-year dashboard lived its five river-valley worlds (1024 cells, the regimes in turn) in 75.8 minutes on four cores, beside other work, and FAILED one row. **Population green**, for the first time: all five worlds keep ten or more after fifty years, with 82 to 87 people from 40 founders (50 to 59 at year 10, 75 to 88 at year 40), the most growth 2.3 times the founders (world 2, year 44), within the bound; the baseline (§9, slice T) had two worlds of five keep ten, and `main`'s nightly dashboards of 2026-10-05 to -07, before soils, kept 0, 2 and 1. Food prices green in every world (grain asked 3 to 7 log points more before the harvest than after, stocks rising after every harvest). The Gini of goods amber: 0.27 to 0.40, two worlds within the band of 0.3 to 0.75 and three just below it, as hoe farmers with land to spare may be (research 08-14 §3.1: horticultural populations 0.27 ± 0.03). Firm sizes green (155 to 273 workshops a world). **Structural failures RED:** 11 failures of lived-in buildings in 4,635 building-years, 2.37 per 1,000, against at most 2 (the baseline: 2.17), and none of empty buildings besides. A world now takes 33 to 76 minutes, the village worlds the longest, as villages hold more people and fields than the baseline's. Slice W's time-boxed tuning answers to the failures; the middens' step came after this run.
- **Accelerated mode's approximations, as built (2026-10-07, slice W, ADR-0011 §4 amended):** measured on a river-valley world lived twenty years (seed 1, 1024 cells; 65 people, 121 fields, 16 roofed huts), 90 to 120 days from its save with `bench_village`. A profile of 30 days at Max with leisure blocks put 24 % of the time in rebuilding a settlement's walking times (a Dijkstra over the whole map, as hunting walks two hours), 12 % in planning routes, 8.5 % in each day's expected yields, 8 % in finding the best purchase and 5 % in finding the best place to gather. About 14.5 decisions are made a person-day. **Exact:** a settlement's walking times are rebuilt when the paths are surveyed or the hearth moves; the monthly refresh on the same survey now finds only the ground that could be broken again. Detailed went from 60.7 to 56.8 ms a day, every section's digest unchanged in both modes. **The household view** keeps what a household's members weigh whoever decides: the purchase, whom to ask, paid work, the best place for each gathering, digging and field activity. It is kept from the household's first decision after midnight until midnight or its own consequential step: a deposit, work on a field or a building, making, trying, or a choice that lays claim to something. Every household's view is refreshed after a gift, a trade or paid work. Alone it made a day 20 % faster (45.6 against 57.2 ms a day over 120 days), with time use unchanged within 0.01 h a person-day. **Leisure blocks** alone made a day 15 % faster (48.7 ms a day); both together 23 % (44.2 ms a day), about 16 s a simulated year. `max_minutes` of rest, play and the hearth (180, tuning values; Detailed mode never uses them) caps a block. Both approximations are set aside at midnight, so Gate A's save at an Accelerated midnight still lives on exactly; with both switched off (`Sim::set_approximations`), ten days at Max still end in the bytes of ten by the minute.
- **Gate B's tolerances (2026-10-07, set before Accelerated mode was evaluated, ADR-0011 §5):** `civ-host consistency --calibrate` lived each fixture (river valley seed 1 and ria coast seed 2, 768 cells, fixed identities, lived by the minute to 1 January of year 3) eight times in Detailed mode for a year, each with its own tie-break stream, in 1.8 minutes on four threads; every run passed the long runs' checks. The river valley barely varies when only the order of an instant's events is redrawn: 44 people in every run, work 2.625 ± 0.005 h a person-day. The coast varies more: 41.6 ± 0.5 people, work 3.00 ± 0.15 h, materials ± 7 %. Each aggregate's tolerance bounds the difference of the two modes' means as a share of the Detailed mean. It is the larger fixture's three standard errors of a difference of means of five runs each, at the Detailed spread, rounded up to a whole percent and at least 3 %. The roofed share's floor is 10 %, one household in about ten. **The tolerances:** people 3 %, work 10 %, leisure 3 %, sleep 3 %, walking 9 %, food 4 %, harvest 4 %, materials 14 %, roofed 10 %, Gini of goods 8 % (tuning: the floors are judgement, as the redrawn tie-break stream is the only spread the gate sees). Widening one needs its own entry here.
- **Gate B's first evaluation (2026-10-07):** FAILED. Walking fell 12.0 % in the valley and 11.5 % on the coast, against a 9 % tolerance; every other aggregate was within its tolerance, and every run passed its checks. Run with one approximation at a time (`--without-leisure-blocks`, `--without-household-view`), the household view alone passed every aggregate (walking +0.0 % and +2.8 %), and the leisure blocks alone did not (−11.0 % and −9.1 %). In the twenty-year valley over 120 days, the blocks moved time from the hearth to rest at home: the hearth 4.20 to 3.86 h a person-day, rest 3.53 to 3.97, walking 0.524 to 0.479. Detailed mode decides again after each session, and its softmax choice may then go to the hearth. A block lasting until the next boundary, followed by a choice that may pick the same leisure again, lengthens every run of the leisure first chosen. The blocks were redrawn (ADR-0011 §4 amended): a block lasts the geometric number of sessions Detailed mode would live before choosing something else, drawn from the chance with which the leisure was chosen; one that ends so makes the next decision pass that leisure over; one cut at a boundary or at its longest is followed by a decision made afresh. Time use came within 4 %: the hearth 4.35 h, rest 3.45, walking 0.507. **Gate B then passed** (20 runs, 1.2 minutes on four threads, every run passing its checks). The largest differences were walking −4.4 % in the valley and the Gini of goods −5.0 % on the coast; people, sleep, food, harvest and the roofed share were within 0.5 %. No tolerance was widened. With the first blocks and the view together, one 120-day run showed trading 24 times as long as in Detailed mode (0.048 h a person-day against 0.002); with the redrawn blocks it was 0.001, and Gate B has no aggregate for trade alone.
- **The dashboard with Accelerated mode's approximations (2026-10-07, slice W, `cd6bfeb`):** the fifty-year dashboard lived its five worlds in 50.0 minutes on four cores, beside other work (75.8 minutes at `269edf3`), and **passed** for the first time: four rows green and one amber. Worlds 1, 2 and 3 took 24 to 25 minutes, world 5 16, and world 4, a village under village fields, 50. **Population green:** all five keep ten or more after fifty years (93 to 104 people in four, 10 in world 4), the most growth 2.7 times the founders (world 1, year 49). Food prices green (grain asked 3 to 8 log points more before the harvest than after; stocks rose after every harvest), firm sizes green (108 to 204 workshops a world), the Gini of goods amber (0.23 to 0.45; four of five below 0.3, as hoe farmers with land to spare may be). **Structural failures green:** 5 failures of lived-in buildings in 4,548 building-years, 1.10 per 1,000, against at most 2. All five were hut roofs whose rafters broke in storms, three of them poorly made, and none killed anyone; 6 empty buildings gave way besides. At `269edf3` the count was 11 in 4,635 building-years (2.37). Counts of 11 and 5 are within chance of one rate of about 1.7, and the slice's time-boxed tuning found nothing in the row to tune: it was spent reading the failures (the dashboard's JSON report now keeps their words). World 4 grew to 95 people and fell to 6 in year 43 (*NUDGE* below).
- **NUDGE: a village of 95 leaves together (2026-10-07, world 4 of the dashboard above):** under village fields, its harvests were 23.9 t in year 41 and 15.7 t in year 42, against about 22 t its 95 people eat in a year. On 14 May of year 43 food ran short with about two days' left, and within two weeks 17 households gave up and left; six people stayed, and ten lived there at year 50. The households-fields worlds of the same dashboard grew through such years. This is *villages of about 55 leave together* (§9, the M3b demo) at a larger size, and the regime may matter: whether the settlement's allocation by need keeps up with a village's growth has not been traced. Revisit with M4's councils, which give a village other answers to a short year than leaving. The chronicle now names the settlement a household leaves; before, it read "gave up and left ." in the observer and in `chronicle`.
- **The M3c demo (2026-10-07, plan §7):** river valley seed 2, the seed of the M3a demo, 768 cells, a band of 40, lived eight years from the command line (`civ-host run`) three times. The seed's weather is the same in every run, and `civ-host weather --seed 2` shows that it holds a dry year 4 (616 mm against 797 usual; 188 mm from April to July, the crop's season) and a wet year 5 (1,058 mm). The seed was the earlier demos', and was not picked for how its village fared. A year in the report runs from 1 March, so each holds its calendar year's harvest. **The dry year:** the crops had 57 to 58 % of the water they needed (a harvest 0.69 to 0.71 of an average year's). The village reaped 8.9 to 9.3 t of grain (502 kg/ha in the first run), against 16.5 to 18.7 t the year before. Its grain stores fell from 13.4–15.3 t to 10.8–12.7 t, and grain was asked at 1.36 to 1.42 hours of work a kilogram, against 1.14 to 1.20 the year before. Nobody went short of food and nobody left: about a year's grain in store carried them. **The wet year:** 1,146 mm in the report's year, the crops had 90 to 91 % of their water, and the village reaped 19.4 to 20.0 t (962 kg/ha in the first run). Stores rose to 17.8–19.7 t, and grain was asked at 1.14 to 1.16 again. The next years were wet too, and stores reached about 30 t by year 8. `web/e2e/m3c-demo.spec.ts` (with `TCE_DEMO=1`) makes the village to 1 November of year 5 and shows it in the observer: the weather panel's years against what they usually bring (year 4 "dry", 616 against 797 mm), the market with 11.8 t of grain offered by 11 households, and the chronicle's months that stood out. It then lives on at Max, the clock standing at a midnight each time it shows. Run once, it lived to year 41 at Max before the run stopped, with 71 people. Then fifty years in Accelerated mode passing the dashboard: the entry above.
- **The dashboard on M3c's last build (2026-10-07, `8093d51`, with nothing beside it):** passed in 31.7 minutes on four cores. Four rows green and the Gini of goods amber (0.26 to 0.33). All five worlds kept their bands, ending with 80 to 92 people (85 and 92 under village fields), the most growth 2.4 times the founders (world 4, year 48). Grain was asked 3 to 9 log points more before the harvest than after, and stocks rose after every harvest. **Structural failures:** 7 in 4,574 building-years, 1.53 per 1,000, every one a hut roof whose rafters broke in a storm, none killing anyone; no empty building gave way. No world fell as world 4 of the run before did. Worlds took 15 to 24 minutes each. Over the two runs of slice W's builds, 12 failures of lived-in buildings in 9,122 building-years, 1.32 per 1,000.
- **M4 in three parts (2026-10-07):** M4's contents need about five decisions expensive to reverse: the polity with its offices and laws, ties and standing, incidents and cases with their obligations, factions and episodes, and interventions that touch beliefs. That is more than one milestone's three ADRs (§1 rule 7). M4 is split into M4a (standing and the first council), M4b (crime and order) and M4c (factions and unrest), each with the usable bar and a demo of its own; M4's demo is M4c's. The council comes first because crime needs offices to answer it and unrest needs institutions to contest.
- **M4a design (2026-10-07, plan §7):** three research briefs (governance and law; crime and order; factions and unrest), with an observer summary, drawn from about thirty reports, their key citations checked against the reports. Three slices, X to Z (§7), and two ADRs: [ADR-0013](decisions/0013-polity-offices-laws.md) (the polity, its offices and its laws) and [ADR-0014](decisions/0014-ties-standing-notables.md) (ties, standing and notables). Choices that depart from the plan's first sketch:
  - **Scored moves, not HTN (amends §4.2).** Deliberation is one level deep: moves that a person's powers and the present issues allow, scored by their forecast effect on that person's goals. HTN planning waits for multi-step plans (M7). Issues only make moves available; no issue carries a weight toward a policy, as "a levy follows a famine" would be plot.
  - **One founding custom for every world.** A gathering of adults deciding by acclamation, where anyone may propose, and which may amend itself. Different regimes must come from history, not from a drawn starting form.
  - **Influence from ties held by others (amends §4.2).** §4.2 ranks notables by office, wealth, followers, founder status and reputation. Here standing is the esteem others' ties hold, by domain and audience; office and wealth count only through acts people saw.
  - **Notables are a compute tier.** They deliberate weekly and others when something reaches them. The flag grants nothing and is read only by the scheduler, and a gate checks it against every adult deliberating (ADR-0014 §4). §4.2's 1 % gets a floor, so a village of a hundred has several.
  - **Leaving becomes a scored choice.** Today a household leaves when food runs short. In slice Z it weighs staying, with what it expects from its fields, its stores, those it can ask and any relief, against going.
  - **Settled now for M4b and M4c:** no corporal punishment or execution in v0; the god tool's whispers are true claims only; an agitator is a newcomer sent with an ideology, not a person rewritten.
- **Scale at 1,000 people (2026-10-07, measured before slice X):** a band founded by `bench_village` on seed 2 (1,024 cells) at Max. 300 people lived a day in 111 ms; 1,000 lived one in 653 ms over their first ten days and 811 ms by days 21 to 30, about four minutes a simulated year, growing faster than the population. A profile (callgrind, 1,000 people, the second week) put 35 % of the time in route searches (about 720 a day at 1.7 million instructions each): the route cache holds 50,000 routes and is cleared whole when full, as well as monthly. 25 % was in scoring candidates, 11 % in trade's search for a deal, 9 % in `exp`, and 5 % in looking for deposits along each walk, which tests every exposed deposit against every segment of the route. Slice X works on these, exactly.
- **Slice X, scale (2026-10-07, M4a, `ce69c6f` and the commit after):** time-boxed exact speed work, each change proven by digests of every save section over 30 days at Max from a 1,000-person and a 2,000-person save (`bench_village --load … --max --digest`). **Changes:**
